# Material del Capítulo 25 — Por qué una audiencia: atención, distribución y leverage

Este archivo reúne TODO el material obligatorio del capítulo, ordenado por sección y clúster según `_libro/04_arquitectura.md`. Las unidades aparecen completas (copiadas de `_libro/02_unidades/`).


# SECCIÓN 25.1 — La atención como poder


## CLÚSTER C-T01a-21: Attention as power (consumers vs creators)
- idea: Quienes están en la base de las pirámides sociales no invierten sobre todo dinero, sino atención. Como sobrevivimos en un nivel conceptual, trabajamos con nuestra atención para sostener la identidad (título laboral, religión). Algo "doesn't exist unless there is enough attention to give it life", y cuando se amenaza esa atención reaccionamos como ante una amenaza física (el insulto a tu equipo deportivo), defendiendo y cableando la creencia. Por eso "power in our modern world comes from attention", tanto externo como interno (controlar la propia atención). La masa de consumidores alimenta la sociedad con su atención y la vida "les pasa", como a "a bird in heavy winds". Unos pocos creadores controlan su destino controlando su atención y expanden la consciencia de otros capturando la suya.
- nivel: fundamental
- evolución: ninguna
- fusiones (unidades sustancialmente idénticas; usa la unión de su contenido): FUSION: U-023-055 <= U-003-127 | mismo argumento sobre la atención como poder, con la misma cadena y los mismos ejemplos (equipo deportivo, modelo de negocio desconocido); U-003-127 es la reaparición en la compilación de 2024

### U-023-055
- **tipo:** argumento
- **titulo:** In societal pyramid schemes the main investment is attention, not money; things exist only with enough attention to give them life
- **desarrollo:** Money plays a role but "isn't the biggest problem": people at the bottom of societal pyramid schemes are mostly investing attention. Chain: (1) humans survive on a conceptual level; (2) if you identify with, e.g., your job title, you'll work to survive that identity with your attention; (3) "it doesn't exist unless there is enough attention to give it life" — a religious ideology, a business model (there could be a business model out there no one knows about because no one has done it or spread the word, so no attention makes it seem tangible); (4) identities (job title, religion) are upheld through attention; if the attention is threatened, the thing's existence is threatened and you feel threatened — an actual physical survival response — and you defend that belief over and over, hardwiring it; (5) zoom out to the bigger picture and those problems mean nothing. Conclusion: "power in our modern world comes from attention", both external power and internal power (being able to control your own attention).
- **ejemplos:** Someone saying "I don't like your sports team" triggering a survival response; an unknown business model nobody has given attention.
- **cita:** "power in our modern world comes from attention"
- **terminos:** investing attention; conceptual survival; external power; internal power
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** fundamental
- **prerrequisitos:** U-023-052, U-023-053
- **fuente:** Society Is A Pyramid Scheme (Take Back Control Of Your Life).md, 9:53-11:30, 2023-01-15
- **tension:** ninguna


### U-003-127
- **tipo:** argumento
- **titulo:** People at the bottom of societal pyramids invest attention, not money; things exist only while attention sustains them, so power in the modern world comes from attention.
- **desarrollo:** "Money isn't the biggest problem the people at the bottom of these societal pyramid schemes are not investing money... they're investing attention." Because humans survive conceptually, if all you know and believe is your job title, "you're going to work to survive that identity with your attention." Things don't exist "unless there is enough attention to give it life" — a religious ideology, or a business model nobody has done or spread the word about, doesn't seem to exist. Identities (job title, religion) are "upheld through attention"; if attention is threatened, the thing's existence is threatened and "you are going to feel threatened" — e.g., someone says "I don't like your sports team" and you have "a physical survival response," defending the belief and hardwiring it, though zoomed out "those problems mean absolutely [nothing]." "Power in our modern world comes from attention" — external power and internal power (controlling your own attention).
- **ejemplos:** Religious ideology; an unknown business model; reacting to an insult to your sports team.
- **cita:** "it's safe to say that power in our modern world comes from attention"
- **terminos:** attention; conceptual survival; power
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** avanzado
- **prerrequisitos:** U-003-102, U-003-126
- **fuente:** Get Ahead Of 99% Of People With Deep Work & Monk Mode.md [1:44:28–1:46:02] (2024-03-21)
- **tension:** ninguna


### U-008-081
- **tipo:** concepto
- **titulo:** Consumers vs. creators: the masses fuel society with attention; a select few control their destiny by controlling their attention
- **desarrollo:** Are you a consumer soaking in value or memes while giving nothing in return, or a creator contributing to humanity with the value you produce? This isn't an internet phenomenon. The masses are consumers: they leech off the government, their parents, the school system, and "blow around like a bird in heavy winds"; life happens to them, is assigned to them; they don't realize they are fueling society with attention. A select few are creators: they contribute more than they take, control their destiny by controlling their attention, and encourage the destiny of others by capturing their attention and expanding their consciousness with valuable content.
- **ejemplos:** "a bird in heavy winds."
- **cita:** "they control their Destiny by controlling their attention"
- **terminos:** consumer; creator; fueling Society with attention
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** fundamental
- **prerrequisitos:** U-008-080
- **fuente:** The 7 Best Internet Business Models (From Zero Experience To Advanced).md, 5:04-5:54, 2024-01-21
- **tension:** ninguna



## CLÚSTER C-T10a-01: Attention is the root of existence (attention economy and attention as ultimate leverage)
- idea: Para el autor, capturar atención es el primer paso de cualquier éxito: no se puede expandir la consciencia de alguien sin capturar antes su atención, y objetar que "capturar atención es poco ético o vendedor" es repetir una idea no examinada. En la economía de la atención el conocimiento es rey (el conocimiento comunica información y la información captura atención), y la atención es escasa: quien domina la mecánica de capturar y entregar valor sobre ella puede cobrar un premium, porque "attention is the ultimate leverage" (Elon Musk, MrBeast como extremos que él no quiere imitar). Jugar el "engagement game" no es malo: es necesario para que otros vean tu profundidad ("you can't change the game but you can change the player"). Cómo: aprender a detener a la gente en el post y guiar su atención por todo el funnel sin ser un "sleazy used car salesman"; y la única forma de desviar la atención de algo dañino es ofrecer algo más persuasivo.
- nivel: fundamental
- evolución: ninguna (posición estable 2022–2026; en 2026 se añade el contexto de la IA que inunda contenido mediocre, ver C-T10a-41).
- fusiones (unidades sustancialmente idénticas; usa la unión de su contenido): ninguna

### U-008-082
- **tipo:** argumento
- **titulo:** Attention is the root of existence: you cannot expand someone's consciousness without first capturing their attention
- **desarrollo:** Question to mull over: how are you going to expand someone's consciousness if you don't first capture their attention? Attention is the first step and "the root of existence"; you must capture attention for any form of success. Objection: "I don't want to capture attention, it seems unethical or salesy" — a word "put into your mouth" that you regurgitate without questioning. Counter-questions: are you mad you're using your attention to watch this? Is it unethical that your attention goes to important things, or to your phone on a notification? Do you want nobody to capture your attention, sit and avoid life, have no meaningful experiences, no highs, lows or story? That's what capturing, holding and delivering value on attention is.
- **ejemplos:** Phone notifications.
- **cita:** "attention is the root of existence"
- **terminos:** capture attention; capturing holding delivering value
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** fundamental
- **prerrequisitos:** U-008-081
- **fuente:** The 7 Best Internet Business Models (From Zero Experience To Advanced).md, 5:54-6:40, 2024-01-21
- **tension:** ninguna


### U-012-188
- **tipo:** principio
- **titulo:** Attention is the scarce resource and ultimate leverage: the game is distribution and curation, not just production and creation
- **desarrollo:** Attention is scarce: limited people and attention to go around. Learn the mechanics of capturing and delivering value on attention and you can demand a premium. AI flooding the space with mediocre content doesn't matter: there isn't enough attention for all of it, mediocre is unwanted, and people shift attention to what matters. "It's not only production but it's distribution. It's not only creation because anyone can create anything. It's curation." "Attention is the ultimate leverage": Elon Musk attracts capital, talent and resources through his followers — his tweets shift markets; MrBeast reinvests attention proceeds into production and is becoming very powerful. These are extremes: he doesn't want to be either (risky, stressful; he doesn't even watch MrBeast).
- **ejemplos:** Elon Musk; MrBeast
- **cita:** "attention is the ultimate leverage"
- **terminos:** attention, curation, distribution
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** fundamental
- **prerrequisitos:** U-012-187
- **fuente:** The Future Of Work (& The New High-Income Skill Stack).md, 16:52–17:42, 2026-02-12
- **tension:** ninguna


### U-018-010
- **tipo:** argumento
- **titulo:** Playing the attention game is not bad; it is required to let people see your depth
- **desarrollo:** Dan stresses "this isn't a bad thing": you "have to play the game if you are going to thrive in this attention economy." Everywhere is somewhere you can catch attention, so "you have to make yourself look good." Reasoning: if you can't catch attention but have a great developed personality, "how in the world are people going to see that personality?" Some of the best people he knows are "talked bad about because they play the engagement game online" and they see "10 times more success than everyone who thinks that that isn't a game that they should play." He later adds: "you're not going to be able to change the game but you can change the player," and you must be a critical thinker who doesn't outsource your thinking.
- **ejemplos:** Friends criticized for playing the engagement game who get 10x more success.
- **cita:** "you're not going to be able to change the game but you can change the player"
- **terminos:** attention economy; the engagement game; the dopamine game

## CLÚSTER C-T10a-41: Attention is the only differentiator (AI, dead internet and engagement vs transformation)
- idea: A medida que la IA llena internet, la confianza, la atención y la señal se vuelven escasas y la sofisticación del mercado sube rápido ("the cookie cutter will change every month"). El autor reinterpreta la Dead Internet Theory como una de las mayores oportunidades para destacar. Pero advierte, adaptando a Daniel Schmachtenberger, que los creadores optimizan para engagement y no para transformación (el algoritmo no mide la transformación), y que la IA acelera la imitación de un pensamiento falso que atrofia el "thinking muscle" y envenena el epistemic commons. La gente no te sigue por información sino por "lore" y perspectiva: cuando una AGI tiene todas las perspectivas no tiene ninguna. Uso práctico de la IA para la marca: conversar con fuentes expertas curadas o destilar material experto en una guía y convertirla en un prompt de blueprint o de coach.
- nivel: intermedio
- evolución: ver C-T10a-28 (tensión con la defensa de 2024 del contenido superficial en redes).
- fusiones (unidades sustancialmente idénticas; usa la unión de su contenido): ninguna

### U-012-163
- **tipo:** argumento
- **titulo:** Attention is the only differentiator: as AI floods content, trust, attention and signal become scarce and market sophistication rises fast
- **desarrollo:** "As the world is filled with more AI, trust, attention, and signal become more scarce." The dead internet is growing; anyone can ask AI for a tweet or make a YouTube video with AI voiceover and B-roll. But that doesn't mean much: they won't get good results or be able to pivot and iterate to compete. It means "the level of market sophistication will continue to increase rapidly": people get bored of the cookie cutter, "the cookie cutter will change every month", people lose trust in most content — and you still must know what you're doing to make AI create something unique and compelling.
- **ejemplos:** AI tweets; AI-voiced YouTube videos
- **cita:** "the cookie cutter will change every month"
- **terminos:** attention is the only differentiator, market sophistication, cookie cutter
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** intermedio
- **prerrequisitos:** U-012-162
- **fuente:** The Future Of Work (How To Become AI-First).md, 22:41–23:29, 2025-05-25
- **tension:** ninguna

### U-014-120
- **tipo:** argumento
- **titulo:** Applied to the internet: creators optimize for engagement not transformation; AI accelerates imitation of fake thinking
- **desarrollo:** The author applies the framework: content creators compete for attention (rivalrous dynamics), so they optimize for engagement rather than transformation, because the algorithm doesn't measure transformation — it measures views, likes, shares. Creators are thus more likely to abandon truth or transformation for whatever makes people react. Engagement-optimized consumption requires no thinking or understanding, so the viewer's "thinking muscle atrophies." AI accelerates content production but also imitation; when there is only destructive content to imitate, "you can see where that goes." AI itself isn't the issue: "it can mimic what looks like real thinking without requiring any cognitive effort from either the creator or the consumer." Default outcome: the epistemic commons is poisoned fast, because it's optimized for content that "looks like it should shift your thinking, but structurally it cannot."
- **ejemplos:** ninguno
- **cita:** "it can mimic what looks like real thinking without requiring any cognitive effort from either the creator or the consumer"
- **terminos:** engagement vs transformation, thinking muscle, epistemic commons
- **origen:** adaptada-de:Daniel Schmachtenberger
- **nivel:** intermedio
- **prerrequisitos:** U-014-118
- **fuente:** I'm begging you to start writing essays (even if you hate writing).md, 9:44-10:33, 2026-04-05
- **tension:** ninguna

- **desarrollo:** "The first thing is you can curate expert information and have a conversation with it": find YouTube videos or books "from people who are certified experts and add those to a chat rather than asking for a general AI's opinion, because AI works best when you know what you want." "The second and more complex one is to take that expert information, break it down into a simplified guide that you'll be able to give to the AI": e.g., take "this guy's personal brand strategy" and ask AI "to turn it into a guide," which "removes all of the fluff," then "turn that into whatever kind of prompt you want." Dan did this for the canvas prompt "with my own personal brand strategy." Prompt types: "spitting out a blueprint or acting as a coach."
- **ejemplos:** Converting an expert's personal brand strategy into a guide and then a prompt.

## CLÚSTER C-T14a-20: Distribution and persuasion separate humans from AI ("attention is one of the last moats")
- idea: La paga depende de la calidad del resultado y de lograr que a la gente le importe, y eso es lo que separa a la IA del humano: la IA puede escribir un cuento o un artículo, pero no tiene distribución (audiencia) ni persuasión, ni dinero propio para anuncios. La IA puede escribir un libro, pero no un bestseller, que depende de hooks, conexiones con quien tiene distribución, estrategia en Amazon y librerías. Quienes temen que la IA les quite el trabajo creativo son, según el autor, los que "weren't going to make it anyways" porque no saben monetizar. Hacia el futuro, la atención es uno de los últimos fosos porque sigue siendo escasa: si cualquiera puede crear cualquier cosa, ganan los productos que la gente conoce. Por eso el trabajo creativo sigue valiendo la pena de pagar.
- nivel: intermedio
- evolución: ninguna
- fusiones (unidades sustancialmente idénticas; usa la unión de su contenido): ninguna

### U-016-004
- **tipo:** argumento
- **titulo:** What separates AI from humans is distribution and persuasion: getting people to care about what you created
- **desarrollo:** Since pay depends on the quality and results of a creation and on getting people to care, Dan argues "this is what separates AI from human": it takes "a lot of knowledge and skill to get people to care about what it is that you actually created." AI can write a short story or an article, but "you are the one that is going to deploy that writing in a place and in a way that gets people to read and care about it and pay you." What AI lacks is "distribution and persuasion." If an AI writes a short story, it cannot get it into other people's hands or get paid: you need an audience on social media, or advertising where you put your own money ("that AI doesn't have") into Facebook ads.
- **ejemplos:** An AI-written short story or article that has no way to reach readers without an audience or paid ads.
- **cita:** "what is lacking there is distribution and persuasion"
- **terminos:** distribution; persuasion
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** intermedio
- **prerrequisitos:** U-016-003
- **fuente:** Working Hard Won't Make You Rich (Do This Instead).md, 1:29-2:17, 2024-03-31
- **tension:** ninguna

- **titulo:** People worried about AI taking their creative jobs are the ones who weren't going to make it anyway because they don't know how to monetize
- **desarrollo:** Following from the previous argument, Dan claims that "people that are worried about AI taking their creative jobs are the ones that weren't going to make [it] anyways because they don't know how to monetize." The worry exists because they lack distribution and persuasion, not because AI can replace them.

- **titulo:** Attention is one of the last moats because it stays scarce; if anyone can build anything, the winners are the ones people know about

You can have the greatest mobile or SaaS app, "but if nobody knows about it, it just ends up like every single other product." "It's never only just been build this thing."

# SECCIÓN 25.2 — Distribution equals freedom


## CLÚSTER C-T10a-04: Anyone can build the thing (creators, coders and authors need their own audience)
- idea: Cualquiera puede construir una app, escribir un libro o hacer arte, pero pocos logran atraer gente y hacer que le importe: ahí fallan programadores, creativos y autores. La alternativa a los gatekeepers (sellos discográficos, editoriales, inversores) es construir una audiencia alrededor de tus intereses para que tu obra sea el producto. Casos: Devon Eriksen vendió por su cuenta su novela Theft of Fire expresando sus opiniones online y considera a la editorial un "useless buffer" que daña el feedback loop con la audiencia; sus ventas son un subproducto de un perfil con pensamiento desarrollado, a diferencia de autores cuyo perfil es solo promoción del libro. Un SaaS sin distribución exige "un maratón" de esfuerzo para capital, usuarios y talento, mientras que con audiencia cada lanzamiento tiene distribución gratuita y no es "one and done".
- nivel: fundamental
- evolución: ninguna (el argumento del SaaS de 2023, anunciado como futuro lanzamiento, reaparece en 2026 como experiencia: si un producto fallara tiene gente dispuesta a invertir y trabajar en el siguiente).
- fusiones (unidades sustancialmente idénticas; usa la unión de su contenido): ninguna

### U-010-249
- **tipo:** argumento
- **titulo:** Anyone can build the thing; few can attract people and make them care, which is where coders, creatives and authors fall flat
- **desarrollo:** "Anyone can build the thing. You can build a note-taking app or a writing app right now if you wanted to, but not everyone can attract people to that thing or allow them to care about it. That's where most coders, creatives or authors fall flat. They build a cool app or they write a book or they create art but they can't get people to care. They can't get customers." Thankfully you can build an audience: "social media is the New Media"; you don't need radio, TV or a billboard.
- **ejemplos:** Note-taking/writing apps.

### U-006-066
- **tipo:** argumento
- **titulo:** A direct relationship with your audience gives better feedback; the publisher is a useless buffer that disrupts the feedback loop.
- **desarrollo:** Devon Eriksen was offered publishing deals once he got out on his own "and people figured out I could sell." His response: "what use are you to me I can have a direct relationship with my audience where I can be more responsive to them and I can be more appealing to them and I can get better feedback about what I'm doing wrong and what I'm doing right directly from them not only from what they say but from their actions." He concluded the value wasn't there; publishing was "this useless buffer in the relationship between author and audience that actively disrupted the quality of the feedback loop." He got pushback at science fiction conventions: when he said publishing houses add no value for new authors, a man threw a tantrum and called him a sociopath — later revealed as an editor from a major publishing house: "I was standing between this man and his bread," and the man had the self-limiting belief "I am an editor from a major Publishing House."
- **ejemplos:** The editor at a sci-fi convention calling him a sociopath.
- **cita:** "this useless buffer in in the relationship between author and audience that that actively disrupted the quality of the feedback loop"

## CLÚSTER C-T10a-06: Distribution equals freedom ("build distribution, then build whatever you want")
- idea: Adaptando "discipline equals freedom" de Jocko Willink, el autor sostiene que en el negocio de una persona "distribution equals freedom" y "audience equals distribution". Distribución es "potential traffic": todo lo que puede enviar tráfico a un producto, servicio o rincón de internet (audiencia, lista, comunidades, la audiencia de tu red). Retoma de Jack Butcher "build distribution, then build whatever you want": con distribución las opciones de monetización son infinitas y las oportunidades llegan solas ("give value, build distribution, ask to get paid, that is it"). Es la palanca número uno: si no creces en redes, esto no es una oportunidad para ti. Incluye una secuencia concreta (posts diarios, networking, podcasts, newsletter, productos gratuitos y low ticket) y un umbral orientativo: audiencia de 10,000, newsletter de 1,000 y red de 100 para un ingreso de tiempo completo en alrededor de un año. Marca y producto cambian poco; contenido y promociones, constantemente.
- nivel: fundamental
- evolución: ninguna (fórmula estable 2022–2024; la atribución a Jack Butcher pasa de cita literal a "adaptada" con secuencia propia en 2023-12).
- fusiones (unidades sustancialmente idénticas; usa la unión de su contenido): FUSION: U-001-133 <= U-007-134 | mismo pasaje de "The One Person Business Roadmap" (2022-12-18) reutilizado en la guía completa "The One-Person Business Model" (2024-02-06): definición de "distribution equals freedom"; se conserva la versión que nombra a Jocko Willink

### U-001-133
- **tipo:** término-acuñado
- **titulo:** "Distribution equals freedom": distribution is the potential traffic you can send to a product, service or content.
- **desarrollo:** "Build distribution then build whatever you want in life." Adapting Jocko Willink ("discipline equals freedom"), he says in business — especially the one-person business — "distribution equals Freedom." A very important concept few talk about; it falls under digital leverage, while everyone focuses on generic social media tactics. From a creator-business lens, distribution is "the potential traffic that you have to send to a product service or just like some crevice of the internet or some internet content."
- **ejemplos:** ninguno
- **cita:** "discipline equals freedom but in business especially this one person business distribution equals Freedom"
- **terminos:** distribution equals Freedom; distribution; digital leverage
- **origen:** adaptada-de:Jocko Willink
- **nivel:** fundamental
- **prerrequisitos:** U-001-128
- **fuente:** The One-Person Business Model (How To Productize Yourself Full Guide).md, 2:53:38–2:54:31, 2024-02-06
- **tension:** ninguna

- **desarrollo:** Quote by Jack Butcher (lived "rent free" 2–3 years): build distribution then build whatever you want. How to start a billion-dollar company: don't start as one; start as one person leveraging technology. Steps: deploy digital real estate until you have ample distribution; write posts every day that live on your profile; network to get them shared so you grow without the algorithm; get on podcasts to build authority; plug your newsletter so you own your audience; give away small free products to grow the newsletter; sell a low-ticket product to get people dedicated to your brand. Threshold: "an audience of 10,000 newsletter of 1,000 and network of 100" — then you can make a full-time income; it takes about a year if done correctly. Then funnel extra cash into whatever you actually want, even a grand idea.

## CLÚSTER C-T10b-08: Distribution isn't optional (personal distribution center / owned distribution)
- idea: La mayoría se atasca en la distribución ("tengo un servicio pero no clientes"): hay que construirla y priorizarla, no es opcional. Se construye un "personal distribution center" transmitiendo las lecciones de tus metas, escribiendo tus creencias y opiniones y enseñando de forma persuasiva. La audiencia propia ("owned distribution") se acumula: 100.000 seguidores dan un millón de impresiones al mes que costarían ~7.000 USD en anuncios o una eternidad por DM; los empresarios de alto nivel lamentan no haber publicado antes. Para quien empieza sin capital, construir audiencia es el inicio más accesible: educar, inspirar y entretener para "prove your value in public". Éxito = algo valioso × cuánta gente lo conoce × que sea valioso para esa gente (ahí entran marketing y ventas).
- nivel: fundamental
- evolución: ninguna
- fusiones (unidades sustancialmente idénticas; usa la unión de su contenido): ninguna

- **ejemplos:** Freelancer without clients; planner without customers.
- **cita:** "you have to build and prioritize distribution this isn't optional"

## CLÚSTER C-T18-05: Distribution through content (inbound distribution and the education-first software strategy)
- idea: "Si lo construyes, vendrán" no es cierto: hay que hacer que a la gente le importe. Stan era "una mala idea" de escuela de negocios (500 competidores, incumbentes con cientos de millones) y solo 2–3 veces mejor al principio, pero se volvió un cohete porque John hacía vlogs en público y conversaba con sus clientes a través del contenido que consumen; la distribución debe llegar inbound por contenido y se escala contratando creadores. En 2023 Dan formula una estrategia análoga para su empresa de software: saltarse la fase "sink or swim" construyendo las redes de la empresa como una marca personal con un avatar, usar esa audiencia y la de otros creadores para un curso cohort-based sobre el tema del software (95% de margen, motor de ingresos del primer año) y así poder jugar a largo plazo con el software ("we want to create a Creator").
- nivel: intermedio
- evolución: ver "Evolución y tensiones" (plan de 2023 frente al balance de Kortex en 2025).
- fusiones (unidades sustancialmente idénticas; usa la unión de su contenido): ninguna

### U-004-082
- **tipo:** caso
- **titulo:** Stan's first lever was distribution through John vlogging in public — the insight that the best way to talk to customers is through the content they consume.
- **desarrollo:** John: "if you build it they will come is not necessarily true for most people"; "no one cares... you have to make people care." Stan was "such a bad idea" from a business-school standpoint: 500 competitors, legacy incumbents who'd raised hundreds of millions, "shitty and clunky and so overpriced" software. Stan initially wasn't 10x better, just "two or 3x better." Yet hundreds of competitors flatlined while Stan became "a rocket ship." Key insight: distribution. Competitors "were just tech bros trying to make money on a hot economy"; John "bled this," posted content all day, and his customers became friends. "The best way to talk to your customers is through the content that they consume and the place that they're already at" — "distribution should come inbound through content." Scaling that: hire more creators to make content and "blow out your content."
- **ejemplos:** 500 competitors; Stan 2-3x better initially.
- **cita:** "the best way to talk to your customers is through the content that they consume"
- **terminos:** distribution; inbound through content
- **origen:** de-tercero:John Hugh (Stan co-founder)
- **nivel:** avanzado

### U-009-040
- **tipo:** caso
- **titulo:** Dan's software company strategy: skip the sink-or-swim phase by leading with an education product built on a branded avatar
- **desarrollo:** Dan is building a software company but taking a different approach. The common route is to build the product, test it and market it to a specific audience; "this works," but he wants to "skip the sink or swim phase." He is aware that churn, developer costs and ingraining software in people's daily lives will be difficult. Instead he plans to take the company to $5-10 million in its first year ("a lofty goal") by leveraging experience gained in modern business: the power of a personal brand and of digital products. Plan: (1) build the company's social media "like we would a personal brand with an avatar as the face of the brand"; (2) use that audience plus "a plethora of Creator audiences" to fuel a cohort-based course around the subject of the software; (3) that education product operating at 95% profit margins is the main revenue driver in year one; (4) then it doesn't matter if the software itself is profitable in year one; they can "play long term and slowly inject it into the Creator economy as the one true software." He notes most businesses have no idea what it takes to build a social media audience so they rely on creator sponsorships; "we want to create a Creator." He can't reveal details yet due to legal matters.
- **ejemplos:** Dan's unnamed software company.
- **cita:** "I want to skip the sync or Swim phase of this business"

# SECCIÓN 25.3 — Formas de leverage


## CLÚSTER C-T10a-07: Built, borrowed, bought (types of distribution and their leverage)
- idea: El autor clasifica la distribución por nivel de leverage. Versión principal: built (redes y lista de email propias, en tu control, la de mayor leverage), borrowed (podcasts como invitado, menciones y guest posts, shares de cuentas grandes, que "derraman" tráfico hacia lo construido) y bought (anuncios, shoutouts y patrocinios pagados). El consejo es enfocarse en lo construido y "deplatform" a la audiencia hacia el email. En otras versiones distingue métodos de bajo leverage y gratificación inmediata (cold email, DMs, ads: si dejas de empujar te quedas sin nada) de los de alto leverage (audiencia propia, red, alquilar audiencias ajenas) y propone combinarlos mientras la audiencia madura; también una taxonomía de cuatro tipos (manual, bot, borrowed, owned). Para un principiante, construir audiencia es la mejor opción: depende de la habilidad, es gratis y accesible, pero lleva tiempo. Trampa típica: construir web, perfeccionar producto y formar una LLC antes de conseguir clientes.
- nivel: fundamental
- evolución: la ubicación de los patrocinios cambia entre versiones (ver "Evolución y tensiones"): en 2022/2024 son "bought"; en 2023-09 son "borrowed distribution"; en 2023-08 "rentar audiencias ajenas" vía patrocinios figura entre las formas de alto leverage.
- fusiones (unidades sustancialmente idénticas; usa la unión de su contenido): FUSION: U-001-134 <= U-007-135 | mismo pasaje (built, borrowed, bought, deplatform al email) repetido literalmente en la guía de 2024; se conserva la versión con la referencia al mental monopoly

### U-001-134
- **tipo:** framework
- **titulo:** Three types of distribution: built, borrowed, bought — focus on built, and deplatform your audience to email.
- **desarrollo:** (1) Built — growing on social media to gather an audience/readership; retain it, then "deplatform them" because social media can go away any time (especially if polarizing or controversial — you could be canceled and lose the audience): send them to an email list ("nobody can take emails away from you"), a community, a Telegram channel, and keep delivering deeper value there. Most important because you're not borrowing or paying; it's in your control. (2) Borrowed — appearing on someone's podcast, being mentioned in a newsletter, a guest post, retweets/shares by big accounts; it spills over into your built traffic. Best way to borrow: a mental monopoly (U-001-135). (3) Bought — paying for ads (Google, Facebook, Instagram), paying for shoutouts (paid podcast sponsorship falls here too); these overlap (paying for shoutouts borrows audiences). Overall, focus on building your own distribution.
- **ejemplos:** Podcast guesting; newsletter mentions; guest blog posts; ads; shoutouts.
- **cita:** "there are three types of distribution there is built borrowed and bought"
- **terminos:** built; borrowed; bought; deplatform
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** fundamental
- **prerrequisitos:** U-001-133
- **fuente:** The One-Person Business Model (How To Productize Yourself Full Guide).md, 2:54:31–2:56:51, 2024-02-06
- **tension:** ninguna


### U-007-135
- **tipo:** framework
- **titulo:** Three types of distribution: built, borrowed, bought
- **desarrollo:** (1) Built: growing on social media to gather an audience or readership for your value; retain them, then "de-platform" them, because social media can go away (especially if you're polarizing and get canceled): send them to an email list ("nobody can take emails away from you"), a community, a Telegram channel, and deliver more in-depth value there. Built is the most important because it's in your control, not borrowed or paid. (2) Borrowed: getting on someone's podcast to promote yourself and products, being mentioned in someone's newsletter, a guest post, a retweet; borrowed traffic spills over into your built traffic (e.g. being shared by a big Instagram account). Paying for a podcast sponsorship falls under paid. (3) Bought: ads (Google, Facebook, Instagram), paid shoutouts (overlaps with borrowed, since you borrow their audience with spillover). Of the three, focus on building your own.
- **ejemplos:** email list, Telegram; podcasts, newsletter mentions, guest posts, retweets; Google/Facebook/Instagram ads, shoutouts
- **cita:** "nobody can take emails away from you"
- **terminos:** built; borrowed; bought; de-platform
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** fundamental
- **prerrequisitos:** U-007-134
- **fuente:** The One Person Business Roadmap (99% Make This Mistake).md, 11:53-14:13, 2022-12-18
- **tension:** ninguna

"You have something that people want; now you need the people side of that equation." - **titulo:** The three types of distribution: built, borrowed and bought
- **desarrollo:** (1) Built: "you build distribution on something like social media and an email list; this is something you own and can promote your value to at any time; it is the highest leverage of the bunch." (2) Borrowed: "networking with other people, showing up as a podcast guest and getting thousands of eyes on you, writing guest newsletters for people with large lists, posting on forum sites or communities." (3) Bought: "sponsorships and advertisements: podcast sponsorships, newsletter sponsorships, Google ads, Facebook ads, YouTube ads." "In an optimal world you would do all of them," but time, money for hiring, and money for ads "at a positive ROI" are limited. Dan's Cortex example: they already have the audience (built), are working with larger creators to build up to a larger launch (borrowed), and "eventually we need to buy distribution in order to reach the scale that we want."

### U-009-045
- **tipo:** framework
- **titulo:** Four types of distribution: manual, bot, borrowed, and owned
- **desarrollo:** A personal brand doesn't eliminate other distribution types. (1) Manual distribution: cold email, cold calls, cold DM campaigns. (2) Bot distribution: purchasing Facebook ads or Google ads. (3) Borrowed distribution: paying for podcast, YouTube or newsletter sponsorships. (4) Owned distribution: building an audience, newsletter, community or list of product buyers. "The lines are blurred between them all," and he admits he missed things like SEO. All have their use cases: the first three "are incredible for testing," good for beginner business owners and give "some form of control over your outcomes." Owned distribution "is a long-term game that scares most people away." Recommended: systemize audience growth over time and leverage other distribution methods to make your desired revenue.
- **ejemplos:** Cold email/calls/DMs; Facebook/Google ads; podcast/YouTube/newsletter sponsorships; audience/newsletter/community/buyer list.
- **cita:** "own distribution is a long-term game that scares most people away"
- **terminos:** manual distribution, bot distribution, borrowed distribution, owned distribution ("own distribution")
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** fundamental
- **prerrequisitos:** ninguno
- **fuente:** Micro Education Businesses Are The Future & How To Start With Zero Dollars.md, 3:08-4:42, 2023-09-17
- **tension:** ninguna


### U-013-095
- **tipo:** fuente-de-tercero
- **titulo:** "Build distribution, then build whatever you want" (Jack Butcher); low- vs. high-leverage distribution methods
- **desarrollo:** Step 3: build distribution. "Build distribution then build whatever you want" — a quote from Jack Butcher. "Distribution is the most important concept you can understand in the digital race to riches," and distribution methods vary in leverage. Freelancers, agencies, local businesses and even startups often focus on low-leverage, instantly gratifying methods: cold email, paid ads, direct messages — still viable but "you are often left with nothing if you stop putting effort into" them. Better: build long-term distribution while mixing the other methods to see profit quickly. High-leverage forms: building an audience (e.g., a 100,000-follower X/Twitter audience); promoting to your network (a 100,000-follower internet friend promotes you to their followers); renting others' audiences (newsletter or YouTube sponsorships to reach millions). "You must learn social media as the skill to tap into the global distribution network." Audience building takes time, so set aside time for low-leverage quicker methods (cold email, DMs) along the way.
- **ejemplos:** 100,000-follower audience; newsletter and YouTube sponsorships.
- **cita:** "build distribution then build whatever you want"
- **terminos:** distribution, leverage, low leverage, renting others' audiences, global distribution network
- **origen:** de-tercero:Jack Butcher
- **nivel:** fundamental
- **prerrequisitos:** U-013-094
- **fuente:** I Had To Learn These High-Income Skills (If I Wanted To Make Money).md, 18:39-20:22, 2023-08-16
- **tension:** ninguna


### U-026-043
- **tipo:** método
- **titulo:** The product route: you need paid ads or an audience; build it with 3 tweets and 10 engagements a day, then promote daily
- **desarrollo:** If you go the product route, "you either need paid ads or you need an audience"; if you aren't aware of these, self-educate. To build an audience (traffic to send to a product): write three tweets a day and engage with 10 people a day to get eyeballs on your profile, or network correctly to get people to share your content so you grow off their audience. Once you have enough followers "or enough of a distribution network", promote once a day, test and observe, and "actualize the vision of your conversion rate per se".
- **ejemplos:** 3 tweets/day; 10 engagements/day; 1 promotion/day.
- **cita:** "you either need paid ads or you need an audience"
- **terminos:** distribution network; eyeballs; conversion rate
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** intermedio

## CLÚSTER C-T10a-08: Digital leverage (access, network effect, income on demand)
- idea: Lo que la mayoría salta es el leverage digital: con 500,000+ seguidores el autor obtiene acceso (puede escribir casi a cualquiera, incluso celebridades, y recibir respuesta), alcance por efecto red (sus promociones llegan a los seguidores de sus seguidores, muchos de ellos creadores con audiencias de 100K+) y control del ingreso (puede subirlo o bajarlo; podría hacer $50,000 en 24 horas, más si cruzara límites morales que no cruza; $139,000 en un Black Friday "justificado"). El tamaño de audiencia multiplica el output: con 10x seguidores se gana lo mismo con una décima parte del esfuerzo, y un tweet suyo equivale a diez mil de un principiante por la iteración acumulada. Con 1 millón de suscriptores cada video vale lo que un webinar; 12 millones de impresiones mensuales en X equivalen a unos $36,000 de anuncios a un CPM de $3. Con distribución se puede ir más allá del negocio unipersonal (startups, software, productos físicos).
- nivel: intermedio
- evolución: ninguna.
- fusiones (unidades sustancialmente idénticas; usa la unión de su contenido): FUSION: U-001-128 <= U-007-126 | mismo pasaje sobre digital leverage (acceso, efecto red, ingreso a demanda, $50K en 24 h, $139K en Black Friday); se conserva la versión con el prefacio sobre negocios creadores de una persona; la absorbida aporta la estructura numerada y su cita

### U-001-128
- **tipo:** concepto
- **titulo:** Digital leverage: an audience gives access, network-effect reach, and the ability to scale income up or down at will.
- **desarrollo:** What most skip: leverage, specifically digital leverage. His picture: 500,000+ followers across platforms (YouTube among the smallest); on Instagram multiple celebrities follow him and he can reach almost anyone and get a reply — an average freelancer can't. With an audience, even without selling through it, he could get in front of a celebrity and persuade them to buy web design in the DMs. Promotions reach ~10x his followers via the network effect: content reaches followers' followers (e.g., if each follower has 500 followers; and many followers are creators with 100K+ audiences, since creators follow each other) — potential traffic compounds. He can increase or decrease income depending on how much he wants to work and on new projects. Marketers who say not to gain many followers are wrong in his view: he could make $50,000 in 24 hours, $100,000+ if he "stepped moral boundaries" (which he won't — "it doesn't feel good"); on Black Friday, when justified, he made $139,000 in 3–4 days. Preface: this is for one-person creator businesses — talking about interests, few or no employees, technology-enabled freedom; not for freelancers unless they want an audience. If it doesn't align with your goals, don't let flashy screenshots distract you.
- **ejemplos:** Celebrity DMs; network effect math; $50K in 24 hours; $139K Black Friday.
- **cita:** "if I really wanted to I could make $50,000 in 24 hours"
- **terminos:** digital leverage; network effect
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** intermedio
- **prerrequisitos:** U-001-127
- **fuente:** The One-Person Business Model (How To Productize Yourself Full Guide).md, 2:45:41–2:48:45, 2024-02-06
- **tension:** ninguna


### U-007-126
- **tipo:** concepto
- **titulo:** Digital leverage: what an audience unlocks (access, network effect, income on demand)
- **desarrollo:** What most skip: leverage, specifically "digital leverage". Dan paints a picture (acknowledging it sounds self-aggrandizing): 500,000+ followers across platforms. (1) Access: celebrities follow him on Instagram; he can reach out to almost anyone and get a reply, which an average freelancer can't; even if he never sold through his audience, he could get in front of a celebrity and persuade them to buy web design in DMs. (2) Network effect: a promotion reaches ~10x his followers because content reaches followers' followers; many followers are creators with 100k+ audiences (creators follow each other), so potential traffic compounds beyond 500k. (3) Income control: he can increase or decrease income depending on how much he wants to work and new projects. He could make $50,000 in 24 hours, or $100,000+ if he stepped over moral boundaries (which he won't, it doesn't feel good). On Black Friday, "when it's justified", he made $139,000 in three to four days. Hence he finds it funny when (social media marketers) say not to gain many followers.
- **ejemplos:** celebrity DMs; $50k in 24h; $139k Black Friday

I deserve more than you"). - **tipo:** dato
- **titulo:** 12M monthly impressions on X equal ~$36k/month of ad spend at a $3 CPM; across platforms ~$100-150k
- **desarrollo:** He gets around 12 million impressions per month on X alone, probably quadruple across platforms. From a Google search ("I don't know how accurate this is"), a low CPM is about $3 per 1,000 impressions, so X alone equals what would cost $36,000/month in ads; if content and ads are profitable, that should produce well over $100,000/month. You can pay $36k/month for the same impressions he gets after building an audience for three to four years: "instant versus long term," which doesn't invalidate either — "do both but aim for the long term." Twitter is now one of his smallest audiences; all platforms are worth ~$100-150k of ad spend. If he can't out-earn $150k/month he's not getting ROI; since the audience is nurtured, understands what he talks about and is more likely to buy, he believes he should be able to make $300-600k/month as one person with growing distribution.

## CLÚSTER C-T14b-24: Code and media: permissionless leverage (full-stack creator, philosopher builder)
- idea: Naval: "code and media are permissionless leverage; they're the leverage behind the newly rich", software y media que trabajan mientras duermes. Media es el mensaje, el front end de internet; code es el back end. Se empieza por uno (programador o content marketer) y con el tiempo se aprenden ambos, "full stack creator". En 2025 el autor lo eleva a "philosopher builder": fusionar los polos (programador/marketer, big picture/detalle técnico, espiritual/práctico, filósofo/científico) porque "a builder has always needed a thinker" (títulos como "design engineer", coders con distribución). En 2026 sostiene que media importa más que code: el valor del código es "relatively objective" ("if it works, it works"), el del contenido es subjetivo ("with content, there is no works"), así que su skill cap sigue subiendo y quien domina el contenido aprovecha mucho más el código por tener distribución. El leverage ya no es el capital sino la empresa y la marca personal (Priestley); Balaji: "physical is now a premium product". No todos seguirán siendo creadores: muchos usan el contenido como "permissionless launchpad" para aprender todas las habilidades de negocio y luego pasar a e-commerce, software o libros. Con IA se ve cuánto poder tiene una persona; los "skill gatekeepers" juegan juegos de estatus.
- nivel: fundamental
- evolución: de "hay que entender ambos" (2022) y "empieza por uno" (2023) a fusionar polos (2025) y a priorizar media sobre code (2026); ver Evolución y tensiones, caso 11.
- fusiones (unidades sustancialmente idénticas; usa la unión de su contenido): ninguna

### U-011-022
- **tipo:** framework
- **titulo:** Media and code: the two things you need to understand (front end and back end of the internet)
- **desarrollo:** To make curiosity practical (full time or otherwise), you need to understand two things: media and code. Media is the message, communication — how people communicate, especially online; TV commercials and shows were media; now "the entirety of what we see on the internet is media" — the front end. Code is the back end of the internet that houses everything; advancements in tech and code allow more creativity, replace some jobs but create new ones (creators, freelancing). Media is how you capture attention and potentially sell something of value; code is the infrastructure.
- **ejemplos:** TV commercials and shows as media; the internet front end vs back end.
- **cita:** "media is the message media is communication"
- **terminos:** media; code; front end; back end

Naval's quote: "code and media are permissionless leverage", the leverage behind the newly rich; you can create software and media that work for you while you sleep. The author adds: a beginner doesn't realize how much power one person has, especially with AI at its higher level (not asking ChatGPT questions or artists angry at AI) — realizing you can build almost anything. People in the Eden Discord build things they always wanted but didn't know existed; critics say they didn't learn to "actually build it", as if this weren't building. - **titulo:** Step 1 — become a "philosopher builder": merge the polar ends (programmer/marketer, big picture/technical detail, spiritual/practical, philosopher/scientist), because a builder has always needed a thinker.
- **desarrollo:** We respect two types: programmers vs marketers, big-picture vs technical-detail people, spiritual vs practical, philosophers vs scientists. "The future demands that we merge these polar ends because a builder has always needed a thinker." A programmer not paired with a marketer almost always gets disappointing users. "A hippie stuck in the sky and a scientist stuck in an atom are both missing the truth." Embodying this duality makes you unstoppable. Evidence: job titles shifting from designer and engineer to "design engineer" (a more complex merged skill stack); creators founding and fueling companies, pairing with programmers; coders understanding distribution and generating millions of views themselves, drastically increasing earning potential, working on their own projects and possibly out-earning any job. AI isn't directly responsible but the general technological shift is.

### U-012-225
- **tipo:** argumento
- **titulo:** Media (content) matters more than code: code's value is relatively objective ("if it works, it works"), content's value is subjective, so its skill cap keeps rising
- **desarrollo:** He believes the media side (content: posts, videos, podcasts, writing published once and seen by thousands or millions) is more important, especially as more people try to do it all with AI. With content you need to know what good looks like; you need education AI can't give because you haven't started trial and error and "you don't know what to ask". "The value of content is subjective": every reader interprets each sentence differently, so there's "no one right way". Code's value is "relatively objective": if it works and users don't notice problems, does spaghetti code matter? (Granting debate about future breakage.) "With content, there is no works." Consequences: (1) content's skill cap will keep rising; (2) people with high content skill put code to much more use because they have distribution — without distribution, how do you get anything you build (code, woodworking, your life's work) into people's hands and get them to care? That's the skill of media.
- **ejemplos:** spaghetti code; woodworker
- **cita:** "With content, there is no works. There is no set result."
- **terminos:** media, content, distribution, skill cap

## CLÚSTER C-T15-31: Forms of leverage: from Naval's list to permissionless leverage
- idea: El autor toma de Naval Ravikant que las fortunas requieren leverage (con la palanca de Arquímedes de fondo) y va reformulando sus formas. 2022: capital, personas y productos sin costo marginal de replicación (code y media). 2024: labor, capital y productos sin costo marginal de replicación; capital como leverage es gastar dinero para ganar más (activos, recursos, información), pero la mayoría está "broke in more areas than finances". 2025: labor (con sobrecarga de gestión), capital (requiere riqueza previa) y "permissionless leverage", la forma moderna más potente (code, media, libros, podcasts, newsletters, cursos), con retornos exponenciales y sin gatekeepers; su contra es que todos parecen hacer lo mismo, y lo que te diferencia son tus intereses e historias. 2026: "code and media are permissionless leverage... that works for you while you sleep"; hoy media significa contenido, y hacen falta una fuente de tráfico y una oferta propia. El cambio de época se resume en "old leverage" (activos físicos, lineales y locales: casa, fábrica, Rolex, oro) frente a "new leverage" (audiencia, marca personal, IP, datos, software: "media, data and code"), que ya no exige capital: "a good idea and the confidence to post it on the internet". Además, los activos digitales son más difíciles de gravar y confiscar si la IA colapsa los salarios: un email a tu lista puede valer lo que la venta de una casa.
- nivel: fundamental
- evolución: renombres sucesivos de la tercera forma de leverage: "products with no marginal cost of replication (code and media)" (2022, 2024) → "permissionless leverage" (2025) → "new leverage: media, data and code" (2026); y la primera forma pasa de "people" (2022) a "labor" (2024). Ver "Evolución y tensiones".
- fusiones (unidades sustancialmente idénticas; usa la unión de su contenido): ninguna

### U-019-139
- **tipo:** framework
- **titulo:** Three forms of leverage revisited: labor, capital, permissionless leverage, with their cons
- **desarrollo:** (1) Labor leverage: increasing time on tasks or outsourcing to others; cons: management overhead, complexity, and you don't have much time. (2) Capital leverage: money works for you via investments; cons: requires existing wealth or something worth investing in. (3) Permissionless leverage: the most powerful modern form; con: most people look like they're doing the same thing (everyone's a coach, creator, teacher now). What people miss about the creator economy: your specific interests and stories make you unique. "We live in the most permissionless time in history. You can just do things." Forms: code, media, books, podcasts, tweets, newsletters, courses; zero marginal cost of replication: build once, sell as many times as you like; one digital product can serve 1 million users. Importance: never has so much power been in the individual's hands; one person or a small team can reach global scale without gatekeepers; digital products need no permission to build or distribute; they often have exponential rather than linear returns on time.
- **ejemplos:** Everyone's a coach/creator/teacher; one product serving 1M users.
- **cita:** "we live in the most permissionless time in history you can just do things"
- **terminos:** labor leverage; capital leverage; permissionless leverage; zero marginal cost of replication; gatekeepers
- **origen:** adaptada-de:Naval Ravikant (third form renamed "permissionless leverage"; Naval not named here)
- **nivel:** fundamental
- **prerrequisitos:** U-019-059
- **fuente:** Harsh Truth You Don't Need To Grind 247 To Be Successful.md [22:48-24:17] (2025-05-04)
- **tension:** In the 2024 video (U-019-059) the third form was "products with no marginal cost of replication"/digital products; here it's "permissionless leverage", including code and media.

### U-019-064
- **tipo:** concepto
- **titulo:** Capital as leverage: spend money to make money (assets, resources, information); most people are broke in more areas than finances
- **desarrollo:** Capital is a more recent invention than labor. Capital as leverage means spending money to make more money: invest in businesses, real estate, stocks or crypto; invest in resources that take work off your hands; invest in information that helps avoid costly mistakes, make better decisions and be more efficient. Problem: most people are "broke in more areas than finances"; they lack money to invest in assets that pay back a reasonable amount.
- **ejemplos:** Businesses, real estate, stocks, crypto, information.
- **cita:** "most people are broke in more areas than finances"
- **terminos:** capital as leverage

### U-014-173
- **tipo:** concepto
- **titulo:** Old leverage vs new leverage
- **desarrollo:** The rules of wealth creation have changed: industrial-age assets (property, factories, machinery, physical goods) are being replaced by digital assets (audience, personal brand, IP, content, data, software) that are incredibly cheap to build, more scalable, harder to tax, and compound while you sleep. "The only requirement to building leverage for yourself in today's world is a good idea and the confidence to post it on the internet." Old leverage: physical, tangible assets — a house, factory, Rolex, fleet of trucks, warehouse of inventory, gold — bought to appreciate or generate income; linear and local (a factory produces so many units per day; a house rents to one tenant at a time). Old leverage can be good, but most beginners lack the capital (thousands in student debt; no Rolex until the business does well); today leverage is no longer only for those with capital. New leverage: intangible digital assets — audience, personal brand, IP, data, systems, software — "the leverage of the newly rich: media, data and code." Content written once can be seen by millions; software built once can serve millions.
- **ejemplos:** House, factory, Rolex, trucks, gold vs audience, IP, software.
- **cita:** "The only requirement to building leverage for yourself in today's world is a good idea and the confidence to post it on the internet."
- **terminos:** old leverage, new leverage, media data and code, compound while you sleep
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** fundamental
- **prerrequisitos:** U-014-172
- **fuente:** The Writing System That Saved My Brain (Learn Faster & Think Clearly).md, 2:48-4:59, 2026-07-27
- **tension:** ninguna


### U-014-175
- **tipo:** argumento
- **titulo:** Physical assets are the first to be taxed and seized if AI collapses wages; an email list is harder to seize
- **desarrollo:** Physical assets will be the first thing governments find, tax and seize if wages collapse — "more likely today than yesterday" because AI can swiftly do that — leading governments to expand social safety nets. Governments are much less likely to seize an email list of 1,000-10,000 people, a library of content or a best-selling book. "You can sell your house for a hundred thousand dollars, but you can also just send a well-written email to your email list and make ten to a hundred thousand dollars if you know what you're doing."
- **ejemplos:** House for $100,000 vs one email for $10,000-$100,000.
- **cita:** "Governments are much less likely to seize an email list"
- **terminos:** email list, digital assets
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** intermedio
- **prerrequisitos:** U-014-173
- **fuente:** The Writing System That Saved My Brain (Learn Faster & Think Clearly).md, 4:15-4:59, 2026-07-27
- **tension:** ninguna



# SECCIÓN 25.4 — La audiencia como activo de confianza


## CLÚSTER C-T10a-05: Audience as an asset vs cold outreach and paid ads
- idea: Con cold email y anuncios, quienes no compran "desaparecen"; con una audiencia conservas el acceso, es gratis empezar, cada pieza calienta a la gente para comprar, puedes revenderle nuevos productos, pivotar sin quedar atrapado en un modelo y, llegado el momento, dejar el trabajo con clientes o lanzar una startup con clientes y candidatos ya presentes. El cold outreach no construye leverage (cada pitch es cliente o nada, sin confianza ni autoridad previas), mientras que con contenido "nothing goes to waste": todo suma reputación, confianza con span y depth, o datos. Con audiencia, la landing page es solo el "final kicker" y desaparece la ansiedad del freelancer que optimizaba cada email. Secuencia: probar ideas con contenido en vez de "echar dinero a un horno"; los anuncios son "steroids" y la audiencia "natural bodybuilding": primero uno, luego el otro.
- nivel: fundamental
- evolución: refinamiento de la postura sobre anuncios: en 2022 la audiencia se presenta como forma de no "shovel money into a furnace" (U-007-110); en 2024 los anuncios se integran como amplificación posterior a la validación orgánica (U-016-023). Ver también C-T10a-07.
- fusiones (unidades sustancialmente idénticas; usa la unión de su contenido): ninguna

### U-009-231
- **tipo:** framework
- **titulo:** Six benefits of building an audience vs. cold email or ads (where do the non-buyers go? they disappear)
- **desarrollo:** You can send 10,000 cold emails and spend $10,000 on ads, "but where do the people go after that?" Non-buyers from your ad "disappear"; you can remarket, "but still the point stands." Building an audience on social media: (1) you keep access to people because they follow you; (2) it's free to start, so you don't need much skill or permission; (3) every piece of content warms up your audience to buy; (4) you can build new products and increase profitability — your audience is something you can "resell to time and time again"; (5) people follow you for you, so you aren't trapped in a specific business model or skill and can pivot; (6) when large enough you can leave client work or build the startup you've dreamed of, already having customers and potential team candidates, without spending hundreds of thousands on marketing and hiring.
- **ejemplos:** 10,000 cold emails; $10,000 in ads.
- **cita:** "where do the people that don't purchase from your ad go they disappear"
- **terminos:** audience as asset
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** fundamental
- **prerrequisitos:** U-009-230
- **fuente:** The Fastest Way To Build A One-Person Business (Beginner Guide).md, 5:38-6:26, 2024-09-15
- **tension:** ninguna

- **tipo:** argumento
- **titulo:** An audience is a referral network at scale; with funnels you're in full control of income
- **desarrollo:** In real life and cold email you aren't reaching a community or network; referrals exist but are limited. When you build an audience to 20, 30, 50, 100k, that's 100,000 people who know exactly what you do and can refer people to you. With funnels in place, you're in full control of your income.
- **ejemplos:** ninguno
- **cita:** "that's a hundred thousand people that know exactly what you do and they can refer people to you"
- **terminos:** audience; funnels
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** fundamental
- **prerrequisitos:** U-007-113
- **fuente:** 7 Lessons From 7 Failed Online Businesses (And Making 7 Figures).md, 28:31-29:32, 2022-06-18
- **tension:** ninguna

- **titulo:** Cold outreach builds no leverage: no prior trust, no visible authority, heavy manual work, each pitch is a customer or nothing
- **desarrollo:** With cold emails and DMs "nobody knew who I was there wasn't any prior trust I didn't have a way of showing my Authority or results from the start I had to prove myself." As one person it's a lot of manual work if you want messages to be personal, not robotic and automated. "Every pitch I sent either led to a customer or nothing at all. I wasn't building leverage along the way."

- **desarrollo:** With social media content, warm outreach to your audience and promotions on the timeline: "I create a reputation over time. I build trust with span and depth, short form and long form content. I can build trust in my audience without having to explain myself to them. I can generate interest in my products without trying. Nothing goes to waste: all content either contributes to data failures or growth successes. As I grow larger I can evolve my brand into something I enjoy doing. I gain rather than lose Freedom with time and effort."

### U-009-207
- **tipo:** argumento
- **titulo:** With an audience the landing page is just the final kicker; as a cold-outreach freelancer Dan was neurotic about optimization
- **desarrollo:** Your audience trusts you more after exposure to much of your content, so "you don't need to have the perfect conversion centered landing page and copywriting because people already know if they are going to buy." You've raised their awareness of their problems with content; the landing or sales page "is just the final kicker that provides clarity." When Dan was a freelancer relying on cold DMs, cold emails and referrals, he was "pretty neurotic" about optimizing his website and funnel; every email felt like it had to be maximized; "I didn't have any breathing room" because every action had so much on the line. An audience solves this: "my marketing is done every single day without me even marketing; it's just content that educates others that builds trust." Optimization after that is a bonus.
- **ejemplos:** Dan as a cold-outreach freelancer.
- **cita:** "your Landing or sales page for your product is just the final kicker that provides Clarity"
- **terminos:** final kicker, breathing room
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** intermedio
- **prerrequisitos:** U-009-205

"Paid ads are like steroids for business and building an audience is like natural bodybuilding; you have to do one before you do the other." Order: first organic audience-building to validate, then paid amplification.

- **desarrollo:** The blue-light failure taught Dan the value of building an audience: you don't have to rely on "shoveling money into a furnace" to test things. With an audience you test ideas via content, see what they actually want, then create a product or service for them.

## CLÚSTER C-T10a-10: An audience is a set of people who trust you (not a follower count)
- idea: Siguiendo a Devon Eriksen, una audiencia no es cuántos te siguen (la cuenta de gatos con 2 millones sigue "a los gatos", no a la persona) sino un conjunto de personas que confían en ti lo suficiente para dedicar 20 horas a tu libro o 45 minutos a tu video. Para construir confianza hay que ser digno de ella: actuar con integridad y en el mejor interés de la gente, crear valor real, regalar parte para que lo prueben y asumir que la gente es inteligente (calidad incluso en un tweet de una frase). Por eso el autor prefiere "readers" o "audience" a "followers", término que "perdió fuerza" (alguien puede facturar un millón con un TikTok viral y 500 seguidores). "Likes ain't cash" (JK Molina): seguidores sin autoridad, lealtad y confianza valen poco, aunque no es binario. La autoridad visible también vende: Dan compró los servicios de alguien con 50K seguidores que le escribió cuando él no tenía casi ninguno.
- nivel: fundamental
- evolución: ver "Evolución y tensiones" (relevancia del número de seguidores): en 2023 el autor sostiene que la gente no sigue cuentas con pocos seguidores (C-T10a-16) y presume la escala como leverage (C-T10a-08); de 2024 a 2026 relativiza el conteo de seguidores frente a la confianza.
- fusiones (unidades sustancialmente idénticas; usa la unión de su contenido): ninguna

### U-006-060
- **tipo:** definición
- **titulo:** An audience is not a follower count; it is a set of people who trust you enough to spend their time on what you release.
- **desarrollo:** Devon Eriksen: "an audience doesn't mean how many people follow you on Twitter." Example: an account called "why you should have a cat" posts cat pictures and has 2 million followers — "they're not following him they're following the cats." "Building an audience is about trust an audience is a set of people who trust you where if you come out with something... they're going to say well I'm going to spend 20 hours reading this book because I trust Devon," or devote 45 minutes to a video about success and "some obscure philosophy of Marcus Aurelius" because you've created enough value for them in the past. You're creating "this group of people who know who you are and they trust you."
- **ejemplos:** "Why you should have a cat" account (2 million followers); 20 hours reading Devon's book; 45-minute Dan Koe video.
- **cita:** "an audience is a set of people who trust you"
- **terminos:** audience; trust
- **origen:** de-tercero:Devon Eriksen
- **nivel:** fundamental
- **prerrequisitos:** U-006-059
- **fuente:** The Most Important Skill To Learn In The Next 10 Years With Devon Eriksen.md, 1:26:50–1:29:13, 2024-12-08
- **tension:** ninguna


### U-015-059
- **tipo:** principio
- **titulo:** "Followers" has lost its punch: prefer "readers" or "audience"; no audience, no business
- **desarrollo:** The second need is a way to attract an audience. "The term followers has lost its punch": someone can go viral on TikTok, make a million dollars, repeat it, and have only 500 followers (topic of a future video on how followers don't matter). Dan prefers "readers," but since not everyone identifies as a writer, "let's just call it an audience," because it's a first principle of business: you need people to pay you for the value you provide. "If you don't have an audience you don't have a business."
- **ejemplos:** Viral TikTok, $1M, 500 followers.
- **cita:** "if you don't have an audience you don't have a business"
- **terminos:** readers, audience, followers
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** fundamental

### U-006-061
- **tipo:** principio
- **titulo:** To build trust you must be trustworthy: act with integrity and in people's best interest, create real value, and give some of it away free so people can sample it.
- **desarrollo:** Devon Eriksen: "in order to build trust you have to be trustworthy you have to act with integrity and you have to act with these people's best interest at heart because they are not stupid." They know when you're only interested in what's good for you, when you're trying to scam them, "when you're trying to do something that 60 other people have done better and you're just going to try to jump on the bandwagon." So you must "be trustworthy and create real value and some of it you have to give away for free so that they sample it and they say aha this guy makes good stuff I'm going to pay him because I want there to be more of this stuff that I give a [ __ ] about."
- **ejemplos:** Jumping on a bandwagon that 60 others did better.
- **cita:** "some of it you have to give away for free so that they sample it"
- **terminos:** integrity; trustworthy; real value
- **origen:** de-tercero:Devon Eriksen
- **nivel:** fundamental
- **prerrequisitos:** U-006-060

- **tension:** ninguna


### U-006-072
- **tipo:** principio
- **titulo:** Quality as integrity: offer real value in everything, even a one-sentence tweet, and proceed from the assumption that people are smart.
- **desarrollo:** Dan Koe observes that every piece of Devon's content, "even something that could be as short as a one-sentence tweet," has a lot of quality and thought, and asks if that drives his decisions. Devon Eriksen: "it goes back to the Integrity thing you can't just try to cheat people... you can't just take pictures of your food and put it on Instagram and pretend you're a success you have to offer them real value and I try to proceed from the assumption that people are smart because I want an audience of smart people because smart people have more money" (said laughing).
- **ejemplos:** One-sentence tweets; the high-production audiobook.
- **cita:** "I try to proceed from the assumption that people are smart"

### U-012-226
- **tipo:** fuente-de-tercero
- **titulo:** JK Molina's "likes ain't cash" — followers matter only with trust, authority and loyalty behind them
- **desarrollo:** Not the kind of content where someone gives Claude access to Instagram and gains 100,000 followers overnight — "practically worthless unless you have authority and loyalty and trust". (Eden can automate posting, but "that's not what we're pushing" — you still have to learn.) JK Molina's motto: "likes ain't cash" — followers and likes don't matter. Nuance: that doesn't make many followers or likes useless if trust, authority and loyalty are behind them "to any degree" — "it's not binary". "Intelligent content creation is much more than just posting rage bait."

No way," and bought. You're more open to people who display authenticity and value online.

## CLÚSTER C-T10a-11: 1,000 true fans (a small audience is enough)
- idea: La mayoría quiere vivir de su trabajo, no ser famosa: basta con unos 1,000 true fans, o menos, según el precio (servicios de $5,000, newsletter pago de $10, productos de $50–150, libros o software derivados). "You need a few hundred people who support your work"; las audiencias rotan (unos cientos se van y otros cientos llegan) y, de los 4M+ de seguidores del autor, quizá unos 100,000 lo escuchan con constancia, más de lo que necesita. Los principiantes creen que es millones o nada: el autor reemplazó su ingreso de freelancer con unos 5,000 seguidores y su amigo Randy factura más de $100,000 al mes con 10,000, porque estudió negocio, marketing y ventas. Cada persona sigue a 300–1,000 cuentas, y un servicio de $2,000 ni siquiera necesita audiencia si el perfil demuestra conocimiento.
- nivel: fundamental
- evolución: ninguna.
- fusiones (unidades sustancialmente idénticas; usa la unión de su contenido): ninguna

### U-012-167
- **tipo:** principio
- **titulo:** 1,000 true fans is increasingly relevant: most people want a living, not fame
- **desarrollo:** "Arguably the most important" point: most people don't want to be famous; even if you do, save that goal until you're making a living. You don't need millions of followers — "about a 1,000 true fans", and honestly fewer if you're good, because of price points: $5,000 per client for a service; $10 for a paid newsletter (he's "on the Substack kick" and finds it promising); $50 to $150 for a product; spin-off products like a book or software, since anyone will be able to code an app that solves a specific problem for buyers to buy again.
- **ejemplos:** ninguno

### U-010-256
- **tipo:** dato
- **titulo:** You need only a few hundred supporters; Dan's 4M+ audience is probably about 100,000 consistent listeners, and audiences rotate
- **desarrollo:** "You don't need a lot of followers, you need a few hundred people who support your work and need what you offer" to replace your income or live a decent life; "if you want to take it further then you have the agency to do so but you don't have to." Not everyone will gain "a million kajillion subscribers," but everyone can have enough to sustain the life they want. Audiences rotate: a few hundred people follow you, after four or five months they take a break, by then a few hundred more follow. "My audience probably isn't 4 million plus people, it's probably maybe 100,000 people that listen to me consistently and that's more than I need personally." "You evolve, your tribe evolves; the internet is a constant flow of attention, and if you aren't in it your creative work doesn't make it."
- **ejemplos:** Dan's 4M+ vs ~100k consistent audience.
- **cita:** "the internet is a constant flow of attention"
- **terminos:** tribe, flow of attention

### U-014-178
- **tipo:** caso
- **titulo:** Replacing freelance income at ~5,000 followers; Randy at 10,000 followers making $100,000+/month
- **desarrollo:** Beginners think it's millions of followers or nothing. The author replaced his freelancing income at around 5,000 followers. His buddy Randy, a fitness personal trainer, at 10,000 followers made well over $100,000 a month because he knew what he was doing: studied, invested in his education, learned business, marketing and sales. People find $100,000/month absurd, but "that's on the low end for business" — they're in the employee mindset of being paid for time, not value, with a poor relationship with money that changes how they perceive it. Just replacing your income is "not far off": 3, 6 to 12 months of focused learning, skill acquisition, showing up daily and putting thoughts out. People drastically overestimate what's required to win, psych themselves out and get paralyzed ("paralysis by analysis") because all they see are out-of-touch celebrities with millions of followers — "cuz that's all they follow."
- **ejemplos:** Author at ~5,000 followers; Randy at 10,000 followers >$100k/month.
- **cita:** "people drastically overestimate how much is actually required for them to win"
- **terminos:** employee mindset, paralysis by analysis
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** intermedio
- **prerrequisitos:** U-014-177
- **fuente:** The Writing System That Saved My Brain (Learn Faster & Think Clearly).md, 7:20-8:40, 2026-07-27
- **tension:** ninguna


### U-011-230
- **tipo:** argumento
- **titulo:** Limiting belief 1: you can build an audience — everyone follows 300–1,000 people; a $2,000 service needs no audience
- **desarrollo:** Every person on social media follows 300 to 1,000 people, even creators — "more than enough to make a full-time income if you know what you're doing," because "your audience is not just your audience" (network effect). You don't need to sell a $100 digital product: "you can sell a service for two thousand dollars and you don't even need an audience to do that," but your profile must show you know your stuff. There are enough growth strategies.
- **ejemplos:** $2,000 service.
- **cita:** "your audience is not just your audience"
- **terminos:** network effect
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** intermedio
- **prerrequisitos:** U-011-029
- **fuente:** The Value Creator (A New Internet Career Path For Intelligent People).md, 21:32–22:09, 2023-07-19
