## CLÚSTER C-T10b-11: Start posting to escape cold outreach (inbound and warm DMs)
- idea: Por experiencia propia, el autor siempre recomienda empezar a publicar: freelancers y coaches quedan atrapados en un ciclo de trabajo manual (cold emails, cold DMs) porque no tienen una marca que eduque y genere confianza por ellos. Con la marca, los leads llegan solos y tu outreach sale desde un perfil con autoridad. Darse cuenta de que las redes ofrecen "clientes ilimitados" (incluso sin audiencia propia, si alguien comparte un buen post) ahorra 10–20 horas semanales y explica el 4-Hour Work Day. El outreach se vuelve "warm": solo contactas a quien interactúa con tu contenido (comenta, comparte, te escribe o llena tu cuestionario). Si vas a cold-pitchear por dinero, no deberías temer publicar gratis: el contenido gratuito va primero.
- nivel: fundamental
- evolución: ninguna
- fusiones (unidades sustancialmente idénticas; usa la unión de su contenido): ninguna

### U-007-013
- **tipo:** heurística
- **titulo:** Always start posting content, to escape the manual-labor cycle of cold outreach
- **desarrollo:** From his experience and failures, Dan always recommends people start posting content. Reason: many people who start freelancing or coaching get stuck in a cycle where they have to do manual labor to get clients (cold emails, cold DMs) because they do not have a brand educating people and building trust and authority without them having to do it manually in DMs every time. So you build an audience along the way. Once you reach a certain amount of traffic, brand loyalty and authority, clients start coming to you. As the audience grows, you productize your service, reduce time spent on client work and sell a digital product, or in self-development spaces (mindset, health coaching) even a physical product.
- **ejemplos:** Dan's planned coffee brand with l-theanine (see U-007-014).
- **cita:** "I always recommend people start posting content"
- **terminos:** productize your service; brand loyalty; authority
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** fundamental
- **prerrequisitos:** U-007-010
- **fuente:** If You Are High Value, Start A One-Person Business.md, 6:04-7:36, 2022-10-02
- **tension:** ninguna


### U-010-007
- **tipo:** heurística
- **titulo:** Start a personal brand so leads come to you and your outreach is backed by an authoritative profile
- **desarrollo:** The solution he found to the cold-email problem, after some success with web design and cold email, is why he "always recommends starting a personal brand": it is "the future." A personal brand lets leads come to you and gives you an authoritative profile, so when you do cold DM people "it is much better than a random email showing up in someone's inbox." He does not say cold email is bad ("it's a very good strategy"); just that for most people it makes "thousand percent sense" to start delivering value via a personal brand.
- **ejemplos:** Cold DM from an authoritative profile vs a random email.
- **cita:** "personal brand have leads come to you and have an authoritative profile"
- **terminos:** personal brand, authoritative profile
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** fundamental
- **prerrequisitos:** U-010-006
- **fuente:** The Most Profitable Niche Is You (Create Your Niche Of One).md, 4:42-5:26, 2023-01-29
- **tension:** ninguna


### U-002-117
- **tipo:** argumento
- **titulo:** Realization 1 — Social media gives unlimited clients at your fingertips, even without your own audience
- **desarrollo:** Problem: "Why was I doing so much manual work to land clients that was taking me more time than the actual client work." Chain: (1) posting content and building a following gives "unlimited clients at my fingertips." (2) Even without an audience: if he writes a good, persuasive post plugging his product/service, and through networking or paid growth has someone share it to their audience of 100,000 to 1,000,000, people click through; knowing "how to capture and guide attention," he can "generate leads 10 times faster and easier than just a manual effort." (3) Building his own audience would save 10–20 hours a week on client acquisition. (4) A following past 10,000 or 50,000 would let him pivot out of client work, saving more time. This is how the "4-Hour Work Day" is real: "I noticed what was taking up my time and then used the thing that is called creativity to create a solution ... you call yourself an entrepreneur but you can't spot a problem and solve it in your life."
- **ejemplos:** Shared post to 100k–1M audience; 4-Hour Work Day video.
- **cita:** "you call yourself an entrepreneur but you can't spot a problem and solve it in your life"
- **terminos:** capture and guide attention; 4-Hour Work Day; creativity as problem solving
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** fundamental
- **prerrequisitos:** U-002-115
- **fuente:** How To Make $1 Million A Year As A Digital Writer.md, [9:39]–[11:12], 2023-10-08
- **tension:** ninguna


### U-009-261
- **tipo:** principio
- **titulo:** Warm DMs, not cold: only reach out to people who engage with your content and already see you as an authority
- **desarrollo:** Online business tutorials usually advise cold emailing or cold DMing with a skill you barely have. The difference here: you build an audience from a content focus and "only reach out to people that engage with your content," who know you and see you as an authority, increasing chances of success "by a lot" — another bonus of an audience: people trust you because it isn't "a paid ad showing up trying to sell them immediately." Whom to DM first: people who comment on posts relevant to your offer; people who repost or share them; people who DM you first (questions or just "hi"); people who fill out your questionnaire.
- **ejemplos:** ninguno
- **cita:** "you're only reaching out to people that engage with your content you're not cold emailing or cold dming"
- **terminos:** warm, the art of the DM
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** fundamental

- **fuente:** The Fastest Way To Build A One-Person Business (Beginner Guide).md, 37:07-38:30, 2024-09-15
- **tension:** ninguna


### U-001-040
- **tipo:** argumento
- **titulo:** If you'll cold-pitch someone for money, you should not be scared to post free content; free content should come first.
- **desarrollo:** People feel like impostors posting content (e.g., knowing a lot about web design but trying to land a first client). His rebuttal: if you're going to make money from someone by reaching out with a freelance service, why be scared to post free content? Free content should come first, as a way of distilling what you learned and organizing your thoughts so others benefit and you build authority to the point of landing a client.
- **ejemplos:** Web designer trying to land first client.
- **cita:** "the free content online should come first"

## CLÚSTER C-T10b-13: People follow people (your perspective on information)
- idea: "People don't follow ideas, they follow people who share ideas": el mismo tuit de James Clear y de un desconocido se percibe de forma opuesta por la relación profunda e indirecta con la persona. Tu audiencia no quiere información sino tu perspectiva sobre la información; con Google y la IA, ya no te siguen por el contenido sino por tu punto de vista, opinión, personalidad e historia, que nacen de las ideas que componen tu worldview. La cadena: la atención es la nueva moneda, la novedad la captura y la perspectiva nueva es tu forma única de capturarla. De ahí que primero les importes tú y después tu producto ("your product is you": ser interesante gratis antes de decir "buy my book").
- nivel: fundamental
- evolución: tensión de formulación entre "people follow people" (U-015-143), "people follow perspectives" (U-022-158) y "people follow the content, not the profile" (U-009-239, en C-T10b-29). Ver "Evolución y tensiones".
- fusiones (unidades sustancialmente idénticas; usa la unión de su contenido): ninguna

### U-015-143
- **tipo:** ejemplo
- **titulo:** "People don't follow ideas, they follow people who share ideas": James Clear vs. a random guy tweeting "habits are good"
- **desarrollo:** Pillar two, authenticity: core beliefs to attract the right people. "People don't follow ideas. They follow people who share ideas." Two people can write the same thing and it's perceived drastically differently. If James Clear and a random guy both tweet "habits are good for you": tens of millions know James Clear, his story, his book on habits — "a deep yet indirect relationship" — so most will like and repost because it's him; his name brings other ideas they already know through which they perceive the tweet. From a random guy, people ignore it or comment "yeah, no shit."
- **ejemplos:** James Clear vs. random guy.
- **cita:** "People don't follow ideas. They follow people who share ideas."
- **terminos:** authenticity, core beliefs
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** fundamental
- **prerrequisitos:** U-015-138
- **fuente:** How To Build A Better Personal Brand Than 99% Of People.md, 11:25-12:53, 2025-06-08
- **tension:** Matiza "people don't follow information, they follow perspectives" (U-015-073): aquí el énfasis es la persona, no la perspectiva.


### U-022-158
- **tipo:** principio
- **titulo:** "Your audience doesn't want information, they want your perspective on information."
- **desarrollo:** The author's tweet: "Your audience doesn't want information; they want your perspective on information. 10 people can share the same idea; only one will say it in a way that changes minds." He says sharing this opinion "helps a lot of people think things through," particularly writers and creatives who feel "this isn't original from me."
- **ejemplos:** 10 people sharing the same idea.
- **cita:** "Your audience doesn't want information, they want your perspective on information."
- **terminos:** perspective on information
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** fundamental
- **prerrequisitos:** ninguno
- **fuente:** You're Not Boring How To Become An Original Thinker (Fast).md [2:21]-[3:15] (2025-01-19)
- **tension:** ninguna


### U-021-224
- **tipo:** argumento
- **titulo:** People don't follow you for content anymore but for your point of view, downstream of the ideas composing your worldview
- **desarrollo:** For writers and creators building audiences, "people don't follow you for your content anymore. There's Google, AI, and plenty of information that can be found at the click of a button. People follow you for your point of view, your opinion, your interpretation, your personality, your story. And all of those things are downstream of the ideas that compose your worldview. The ideas you use to make sense of the world." Example: in a conversation about the nature of reality, his mind jumps to history, integral theory, Spiral Dynamics, "because I've deemed these the most useful lens ... It makes the most sense out of the other ideas I've heard that tend to fall flat."
- **ejemplos:** The author's lenses on the nature of reality: history, integral theory, Spiral Dynamics.
- **cita:** "all of those things are downstream of the ideas that compose your worldview"
- **terminos:** point of view, worldview
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** fundamental
- **prerrequisitos:** U-021-223
- **fuente:** How To Learn Anything 10x Faster Than Anyone.md, 33:09–34:18, 2026-08-09
- **tension:** ninguna


### U-014-027
- **tipo:** argumento
- **titulo:** Attention is the new currency, novelty grabs it, novel perspectives are your unique way to grab it
- **desarrollo:** Chain: attention is the new currency -> novelty is what grabs attention -> novel perspectives are your unique way of grabbing attention. This goes beyond entrepreneurship into everyday life: to be remembered, stick in people's heads, and be seen as valuable, you can't just regurgitate what you imitated previously; you must view things from a new perspective. Applies to content, sales, anything you put out. Process: consume based on curiosity -> connect the dots -> from your perspective put out a fresh, novel perspective/message "that makes sense to a group of people that want to follow you."
- **ejemplos:** References "media and code" from the previous video.
- **cita:** "attention is the new currency novelty is what grabs attention novel perspectives are your unique way of grabbing attention"
- **terminos:** novel perspective, attention is the new currency
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** fundamental
- **prerrequisitos:** U-014-022
- **fuente:** Stop Trying To Be Unique... Do This Instead.md, 17:11-18:06, 2022-05-28
- **tension:** ninguna


### U-025-111
- **tipo:** principio
- **titulo:** People don't care about your book or product at first; they care about you first, then buy because they like you — so post your beliefs and opinions and teach about your interests.
- **desarrollo:** "You need to post your beliefs opinions and teach people about your interests." They care about you first, then purchase. This differs from past marketing (radio, billboards — though radio had some personal connection); the advertising industry makes marketing feel "icky" when it doesn't have to be: "you can just talk about what you want and provide something valuable."
- **ejemplos:** radio; billboards
- **cita:** "they don't care about your book or product at first they care about you first"
- **terminos:** creator
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** fundamental

- **tension:** ninguna


### U-006-062
- **tipo:** advertencia
- **titulo:** The "buy my book" mistake: online, your product is you — your ability to be interesting — and there is no shortcut.
- **desarrollo:** Devon Eriksen sees many indie fiction authors whining about lack of success whose every Twitter post is "some iteration of buy my book," and none of it is "let me say something interesting right here right now to you for free to prove that I am an interesting person who can entertain you if you buy my paid products." "If you're on the internet and that's how you make your money your product is you your ability to be interesting your ability to be informative your ability to be entertaining," making something where people spend time and "walk away saying I liked that that was a good use of my time I want more of that." "There's no shortcut there's no here's the six bullet points to a successful internet influencer business where you can make millions of dollars going to cafes and taking pictures of your food" — people may click on that "but they don't care." "Oh my books aren't selling write something interesting."
- **ejemplos:** Indie authors' "buy my book" feeds; cafe/food-photo influencer promises.
- **cita:** "your product is you"
- **terminos:** buy my book; your product is you
- **origen:** de-tercero:Devon Eriksen
- **nivel:** fundamental
- **prerrequisitos:** U-006-061
- **fuente:** The Most Important Skill To Learn In The Next 10 Years With Devon Eriksen.md, 1:30:17–1:32:42, 2024-12-08
- **tension:** ninguna



## CLÚSTER C-T10b-14: Trust is the business strategy ("money is a measure of trust")
- idea: El panorama online se desplaza de las tácticas (funnels, clickbait, urgencia, contadores) a la confianza, la simplicidad y la individualidad: ganan quienes tienen "a personal brand with a personal perspective and time in the game". El contenido y la calidad de las ideas a lo largo del tiempo crean una marca en la que la gente no puede evitar confiar: esa es toda la estrategia, porque "money is a measure of trust". En la era de la IA, con contenido infinito, la gente recurrirá a quien confía: una marca personal que casi pueden verificar como humana.
- nivel: fundamental
- evolución: ninguna (la tesis se mantiene de 2022 a 2026; en 2026 se aplica al contexto de la IA).
- fusiones (unidades sustancialmente idénticas; usa la unión de su contenido): ninguna

### U-014-030
- **tipo:** argumento
- **titulo:** The online landscape is shifting from tactics to trust, simplicity and individuality
- **desarrollo:** The author sees the online landscape shifting: a few years ago it was all funnels, sales strategies, marketing, clickbait; people have caught on. Parallel with corporations vs individuals (from his previous video): communities, unlike corporations, are made of individuals pursuing what they want under a community's hierarchy of goals. People who "double down on trust, simplicity and individuality" make more money and bigger impact than those doubling down on tactics (urgency, countdown timers). Mapping: trust by being authentic; simplicity by making sense to others via a novel perspective; individuality by taking responsibility for your own future. In short: "a personal brand with a personal perspective and time in the game."
- **ejemplos:** Urgency and countdown timers as tactics.
- **cita:** "if you can double down on trust simplicity and individuality then you will win"
- **terminos:** trust, simplicity, individuality, personal brand, time in the game
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** fundamental
- **prerrequisitos:** U-014-027
- **fuente:** Stop Trying To Be Unique... Do This Instead.md, 19:52-20:50, 2022-05-28
- **tension:** ninguna


### U-015-137
- **tipo:** principio
- **titulo:** Forget niche, bio and banner for now: content and quality of ideas over time create trust; "money is a measure of trust"
- **desarrollo:** "Forget about your niche for now. Forget about your bio and banner." They're important, but plenty of people with no bio and a blank profile picture do fine. "Your content and the quality of ideas you post over a long enough time period are what create a brand that people can't help but trust. That's your entire business strategy. Trust. Money is a measure of trust."
- **ejemplos:** Accounts with no bio and blank profile picture doing fine.
- **cita:** "Money is a measure of trust"
- **terminos:** trust
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** fundamental
- **prerrequisitos:** ninguno
- **fuente:** How To Build A Better Personal Brand Than 99% Of People.md, 6:45, 2025-06-08
- **tension:** ninguna


### U-016-276

- **desarrollo:** "In the age of AI, when content can be endless and it's going to be flooding social media, what are people going to turn to? They're going to turn to who they trust. Who is that? A personal brand who they can almost verify is a human, and they can tell whether or not they're using AI in a non-tasteful way."
- **ejemplos:** ninguno

## CLÚSTER C-T11-25: Nobody Wants To Follow A Glorified Search Engine (people follow people, not topics)
- idea: Hablar de una sola habilidad no te vuelve más autoritativo: "at best, you'll look like a glorified search engine of actionable advice", sin profundidad ni personalidad, que es justo lo que el futuro exige. Los creadores que crees de un solo nicho hablan de todo porque son humanos (el creador de fitness que habla de ser padre). Vitali (Stan) coincide: "people follow people, not topics"; un "Book of Knowledge" de un tema no es distinto de un libro de texto; propone ~70% de contenido de expertise más observaciones personales, pero manteniendo consistencia para que todos puedan describir qué haces.
- nivel: intermedio
- evolución: ninguna; nótese que Vitali (U-004-029) mantiene una cuota de consistencia temática que matiza la libertad total que proponen otros clústeres.
- fusiones (unidades sustancialmente idénticas; usa la unión de su contenido): ninguna

### U-027-156
- **tipo:** advertencia
- **titulo:** Niche down too far and you become a glorified search engine lacking depth and personality
- **desarrollo:** In business, niching down for too long or too far makes you "a glorified search engine"—lacking depth and personality, the thing the future requires. People have explored niche areas enough; you needn't subscribe to one path (biologist, chemist); readily available courses, content and books let you become everything. It all points to the creator economy—distributing value you've cultivated over time, though most people don't cultivate it.
- **ejemplos:** ninguno
- **cita:** "you just become a glorified search engine"
- **terminos:** glorified search engine; Niche down
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** intermedio
- **prerrequisitos:** U-027-155
- **fuente:** The Cure To A Mediocre Life (Become Multidimensionally Jacked).md, 9:08–9:57, 2023-08-30

- **desarrollo:** Dan Koe: "nobody wants to follow a glorified search engine that just talks about the same thing all the time." Many creators fear branching into new interests. Test: "look at everyone you follow are they talking about one thing some will say yes but are they really or are they talking about their opinions their interests their beliefs what are they posting to their story." A fitness creator also talks about family life and what he learned as a dad. "They're talking about everything and you think they're in One Singular Niche when they're not because they're human."

"People follow people in my opinion, not necessarily topics." Personality nuance "inspires followership": "this is my area of expertise and 70% of my content is going to be about that, but there's also some personal and there's also some observations." Still, stay consistent with your niche because "people need to know what you're about" and "everyone needs to be able to describe what it is that you actually do"; "but if that's the only thing you do then that's going to be boring." "We got to stick to it but we got to do so much more."

## CLÚSTER C-T17-16: The Twitter epiphany (from cold outreach to inbound)
- idea: El autor odiaba las redes y había borrado Twitter durante años creyéndolo un lugar de política y memes. A comienzos de 2019, cansado de 2–4 horas diarias de cold outreach que no podía delegar, vio en Twitter a gente que simplemente escribía (sin imágenes ni edición, "como mandar mensajes a un enorme chat grupal") sobre cosas que él sabía, que atraía clientes de diseño web y vendía cursos sin outreach, y que lo hacía sin la jerga corporativa. Un tuit de autoayuda de Jose Rosado lo llevó a "Money Twitter". La pregunta "why am I not in their position?" lo hizo publicar: la escritura y la audiencia fueron "the key to my freedom", lo sacaron del trabajo freelance y convirtieron cada publicación en aprendizaje.
- nivel: complementario
- evolución: detalles variables del redescubrimiento (diez años vs ocho años sin Twitter; detonante: Rosado vs diseñadores web vs meses de scroll), compatibles entre sí.
- fusiones (unidades sustancialmente idénticas; usa la unión de su contenido): ninguna

### U-015-004
- **tipo:** historia
- **titulo:** Dan's failed attempts at building a name before Twitter: fitness YouTube, digital art Instagram, photography, agencies
- **desarrollo:** Dan tried to build a name for himself many times: first a fitness YouTube channel, then a digital art page on Instagram, a lot of photography (visible if you scroll to the bottom of his Instagram), then multiple agencies or freelancing businesses while testing tactics for growing and selling on social media. Then he "stumbled across Twitter." He had downloaded Twitter in about 2011, thought it was a place "for like politics and half-baked memes," saw nothing he liked, and deleted the app "for like 10 years." He argues that most people still do not think of Twitter as a serious opportunity.
- **ejemplos:** Fitness YouTube channel, digital art Instagram page, photography, agencies/freelancing businesses; downloading and deleting Twitter in 2011.
- **cita:** "a place for like politics and half-baked memes"
- **terminos:** ninguno
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** complementario
- **prerrequisitos:** ninguno
- **fuente:** The Non-Needy Networking Process (How To Make High Value Connections).md, 1:33, 2023-02-12
- **tension:** ninguna


### U-014-139
- **tipo:** historia
- **titulo:** Early 2019 Twitter epiphany: people just writing, attracting clients without cold outreach
- **desarrollo:** After months scrolling Twitter in early 2019 he realized people were just writing — no images, designs or time-consuming video editing — "like sending texts to a huge public group chat." They wrote about things he already knew ("I could write that tweet"); they used profiles to attract clients, some with web design; people were cool, without the business speak and professionalism he despised, yet "still cashing out big." They weren't sending cold emails or doing omnichannel outreach on LinkedIn that took him 2-4 hours a day — meaningless work he couldn't outsource (young, not much money, didn't want a team). It clicked: people can attract a following and land clients or customers from that audience.
- **ejemplos:** ninguno
- **cita:** "like sending texts to a huge public group chat"
- **terminos:** building an audience
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** complementario
- **prerrequisitos:** U-014-138
- **fuente:** How Smart Creators Will Grow An Audience From Zero In 2025.md, 6:05-6:50, 2024-10-20
- **tension:** ninguna


### U-004-090
- **tipo:** historia
- **titulo:** Dan started on Twitter after seeing web designers giving basic advice, landing clients and selling courses: "Why am I not in their position?"
- **desarrollo:** Scrolling Twitter ("I don't know why... of all places"), he saw web designers and people talking about his interests, giving "basic advice" like "here's the top 10 skills that you need to learn if you want to quit your job." It resonated, and they were landing web design clients and selling courses on web design and freelancing. "I can do the same thing. They're saying all of the things that I know how to say and they're doing really well. Why am I not in their position?" So he started posting.
- **ejemplos:** "Top 10 skills you need to learn if you want to quit your job."
- **cita:** "They're saying all of the things that I know how to say and they're doing really well. Why am I not in their position?"
- **terminos:** ninguno

- **fuente:** How To Grow An Audience If You Have 0 Followers (It's Only 2 Habits).md, 2:52-3:38, 2026-05-14

### U-010-012
- **tipo:** historia
- **titulo:** Dan discovered "Money Twitter" through a self-improvement tweet from Jose Rosado
- **desarrollo:** The agency was neither his first nor last business attempt. Along the way he started logging onto Twitter more, and a self-improvement "harsh truth" tweet from Jose Rosado reached him. He realized "Twitter isn't all memes," people give out value there. After following Rosado, more people showed up in his feed and he was exposed to "Money Twitter," with people giving out incredible advice he was "just taking left and right." Eventually it made sense to start a personal brand on Twitter.
- **ejemplos:** Jose Rosado's harsh-truth tweet; Money Twitter.
- **cita:** "oh Twitter isn't all memes ... I can actually find people giving out value on Twitter"

### U-010-100
- **tipo:** historia
- **titulo:** Rediscovering Twitter after eight years: a web designer posting self-improvement made Dan question his outreach-only mindset
- **desarrollo:** Dan had deleted Twitter for about eight years (he had downloaded it once at a movie theater, followed two people, thought "this app sucks"). Logging back on, a tweet caught his eye: a guy (Jose Rosado) selling web design services, which Dan was also selling (web design and funnels to service businesses), who also talked about self-improvement, mindset and harsh truths. At the time Dan was "conditioned into the mind that cold calls cold emails direct Outreach was really the only way to grow" without paid ads. After years of trial and error, he had quit his job and was full time, making "six eight ten occasionally" [thousand a month], wondering how others made $100,000 a month. He felt he couldn't scale beyond one person: outreach took two hours a day and client work the rest. Seeing people land clients from their Twitter promotions made things click. He notes that personal branding and audience building are kind of a necessity for his four-hour workday.
- **ejemplos:** Jose Rosado; two hours a day of outreach.
- **cita:** "how are these people making a hundred thousand dollars a month and I'm over here making six eight ten occasionally"
- **terminos:** four hour work day
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** complementario
- **prerrequisitos:** ninguno
- **fuente:** Niche Down Is Terrible Advice (For Smart People).md, 4:22-6:25, 2023-07-04
- **tension:** ninguna


### U-014-134
- **tipo:** historia
- **titulo:** From hating social media to it being "the key to my freedom"
- **desarrollo:** "Follower count doesn't matter anymore" and smart people are building audiences a different way. The author prefaces with his story: in the past he absolutely hated social media and didn't want to be part of it; now it's one of the most meaningful and fulfilling things in his life, while most people still think it's toxic. It was the key to his freedom: got him out of freelance work, let him do what he wants full time, gave him his time back. He started writing on social media because he wanted to do what he wants; writing wasn't his first attempt at freedom, and before that he didn't realize writing — "the skill for English majors and academics and technical writers" — was the ticket.
- **ejemplos:** ninguno
- **cita:** "it was the key to my freedom"
- **terminos:** freedom
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** complementario
- **prerrequisitos:** ninguno
- **fuente:** How Smart Creators Will Grow An Audience From Zero In 2025.md, 0:03-0:43, 2024-10-20
- **tension:** ninguna

- **tipo:** historia

- **desarrollo:** "I was once a freelance web designer who failed at every business prior to that and I started on social media because I was tired of doing cold Outreach to land freelance clients." He realized the power of writing for getting in front of more people, also on landing pages: people went to his profile, read content, visited his website — "I didn't have to get rejected as much for reaching out to people cold I just shared what I knew and what I was learning in web design and what I was building." Programmers and designers sharing work gain a following if it's good; if not, "you have direct feedback." For him it wasn't just about freelance work: "every time I hit post it felt like I had learned something new."

# SECCIÓN 25.5 — Las redes como nueva sociedad


## CLÚSTER C-T10a-13: Social media is the new Digital Society (a tool, a new way of life, the path of high agency)
- idea: Pocos ven las redes como "the new Digital Society": son la consciencia colectiva que difunde ideas que la gente adopta como identidad, descentralizada porque la componen creadores que pueden cambiar la dirección de la evolución humana. El perfil es tu "digital storefront" gratuito, donde está la atención; los creadores están al frente de la reputación de las empresas y son las únicas cuentas que la gente sigue por conexión humana. Internet es "el camino de alta agencia": no necesitas permiso para crear, y la respuesta atemporal es compartir el valor que adquieres donde la gente correcta pueda encontrarlo; es una escalera cuyos peldaños creas tú. Implicaciones: es más poderoso construir un negocio o una audiencia que solo votar; como no eres "un monje en el bosque", cura tu feed hacia los "pockets of the internet" que construyen la sociedad que quieres. Matiz crítico: el autor cree que no estamos hechos para que cualquiera pueda contactarnos y que las secciones de comentarios son la parte más tóxica. En su versión más especulativa, la humanidad "trasciende al plano mental" y la marca personal es tu avatar online.
- nivel: intermedio
- evolución: ninguna explícita; la visión entusiasta de 2023–2025 convive en 2026 con una crítica a la toxicidad de los comentarios y a la accesibilidad ilimitada (U-024-164).
- fusiones (unidades sustancialmente idénticas; usa la unión de su contenido): ninguna

### U-012-025
- **tipo:** concepto
- **titulo:** Social media is the new Digital Society and the collective consciousness that spreads identities
- **desarrollo:** Few recognize social media as "the new Digital Society"; most see it as entertainment or a place to complete work tasks. In reality media shapes culture, society and economy; it's how we learn, get new information and communicate the value of our businesses; it creates a social fabric civilization relies on. Social media is "the collective consciousness that spreads ideas that people can adopt as their identity" — like society, it "creates the most children or selves". But unlike government and society, it's composed of creators, so it isn't centralized; creators can change the direction of human evolution. A creator can message millions instantly; 50 years ago reaching a million would have taken months. Social media changes how we spend money because it changes which messages are adopted.
- **ejemplos:** sending a message to a million people 50 years ago vs now
- **cita:** "social media is the collective Consciousness that spreads ideas that people can adopt as their identity"
- **terminos:** Digital Society, collective consciousness
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** intermedio
- **prerrequisitos:** U-012-017, U-012-024
- **fuente:** The Future Of Work Is Play (How To Create A Digital Career).md, 19:40–21:29, 2023-12-31
- **tension:** ninguna


### U-013-093
- **tipo:** opinión
- **titulo:** Humanity is transcending to the mental plane of existence through the internet; the personal brand is your online avatar
- **desarrollo:** The future and now are online, "in the collective consciousness where we're documenting our mind," slowly becoming more virtual — "we're transcending to the mental plane of existence through the internet." He calls this "the next phase of evolution": automating our physical bodies to interact with much less restriction than in the physical world; imagine reading someone's thoughts (with laws imposed). He is excited to see what happens "when we solve the hard problem of consciousness and can literally hack" the "relative world" via its underlying fabric. Hence "your personal brand is your online character or your online avatar." If you don't want to code, that's fine: tools let you build a website, membership dashboard, use Zapier to hook everything together.
- **ejemplos:** Reading someone's thoughts; Zapier.
- **cita:** "we're transcending to the mental plane of existence through the Internet"
- **terminos:** collective consciousness, mental plane of existence, online avatar, hard problem of consciousness, relative world
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** complementario
- **prerrequisitos:** ninguno
- **fuente:** I Had To Learn These High-Income Skills (If I Wanted To Make Money).md, 16:14-17:55, 2023-08-16
- **tension:** ninguna

- **titulo:** Why it's hard to argue against creating content: creators front company reputations, get first pick at jobs, sponsorships and attention
- **desarrollo:** Understand the Digital Society before building real estate. Creators are at the forefront of large company reputations; founders and executives have personal brands (like Elon); creators get first pick at good jobs because they prove their value in content; creators work for other creators to gain experience and leverage; companies come to creators first as sponsors for niche markets; creators are "the only accounts consumers actively follow for human connection not memes or company updates". Theme pages and company pages are "practically useless distractions" unless followed for updates or a laugh.
- **ejemplos:** Elon's personal brand
- **cita:** "creators are the only accounts consumers actively follow for human connection"
- **terminos:** Digital Society

The digital world is accessible and a social profile costs $0. It's where you attract an audience or traffic to your storefront, and it's necessary. ### U-024-094
- **tipo:** argumento
- **titulo:** It's easier to submit a vote than to change your life: zoom out and build a business or audience to bring beliefs into reality
- **desarrollo:** People get up in arms about gas prices or reproductive rights; Dan Koe says he sees both perspectives ("the highest perspective is all perspectives"). His point: "most people hide behind the fact that it's easier to submit a vote than it is to change your life". They can't zoom out to see a more effective way to bring their beliefs into reality. If you worry about gas prices, why haven't you developed a plan to build a business so you never worry about gas prices again? That's "10 times more powerful" for you and others than one "measly vote that may not even get counted". And you could build an audience and present your perspective to millions to actually influence votes. "That's how they keep you locked into this thing": keeping your focus narrowed on their vision instead of you zooming out and crafting a plan. Whatever your beliefs, take it into your hands as much as you can, develop a vision and actualize it, and forget what you have very little control over.
- **ejemplos:** Gas prices; bodily rights debates.
- **cita:** "most people hide behind the fact that it's easier to submit a vote than it is to change your life"
- **terminos:** zoom out; highest perspective
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** complementario

So, if I don't respond to an email that you sent, that's why." ## CLÚSTER C-T10a-42: The internet is the great attractor (serendipity and luck surface area)
- idea: Publicar un tweet es lanzar una idea al vacío: puede terminar en otro lado de internet y atraer a las personas correctas (John Cena siguiendo a Dan, DJs que admiraba, Chris Lovato escribiéndole); "the internet is the great attractor". Según Jason Roberts, la "luck surface area" es proporcional a hacer algo que te apasiona por el número de personas a quienes se comunica eficazmente. El contenido mantiene el contacto con conocidos lejanos que saben mucho de ti (Vitali, de Stan, en un evento en Toronto), y "it might happen overnight but there's like years building up to it". Así se conocen personas afines imposibles de encontrar en la vida cotidiana (Justin Scott y Joey) y amistades como la de Dan y Dickie Bush, surgida de un DM tras un podcast donde se revelaron intereses comunes más allá del nicho.
- nivel: complementario
- evolución: ninguna.
- fusiones (unidades sustancialmente idénticas; usa la unión de su contenido): ninguna

- **desarrollo:** Dan cites "Justin Scott" (as transcribed; possibly the friends Justin and Scott mentioned later) saying "the internet is the great attractor." Dan's own image: "whenever you tweet you're kind of putting it out into a void" — you can assume what engagement you'll get or who will read it, but it could end up on an entirely different side of the internet because someone found it interesting. Ideas put into the void "circulate get better with time and attract the right people." The consequence: unexpected attraction of people you admire, clients, friends.
- **ejemplos:** John Cena following Dan out of nowhere; DJs Dan had followed for years; Chris Lovato DMing Dan "dude I love your content" and talking for a good amount of time.

### U-004-023

He learned that creating content "allows you to stay in touch with people" you're somewhat connected to: "you may not be as connected with them because you don't know as much about them but they know a lot about you," which "creates more opportunity... to have a conversation." Replying to comments builds relationships in which people "continue to support you... on your journey," and "all of these compound." He doesn't believe virality or overnight success is the main thing: "it's just consistent actions performed over and over again... it might happen overnight but there's like years building up to it."

## CLÚSTER C-T10b-01: Social media is a new society (the new town square)
- idea: Las redes sociales no son "solo una app": son la nueva plaza pública (town square) de una sociedad digital, una "virtual reality" en la que ya vivimos y de la que dependen el aprendizaje, la contratación, la enseñanza y la socialización. Las personal brands son la "gente" o los "avatars" de esa sociedad; quien solo consume está dentro de ella sin contribuir (como los humanos de WALL-E). El autor sostiene que ya eres un content creator "con una fracción del leverage" y que descuidar las redes es descuidar el camino evolutivo hacia tu potencial; por eso "creator" debe entenderse como la esencia de tu ser y no como un trabajo de internet.
- nivel: fundamental
- evolución: formulación estable 2022–2024; la metáfora cambia de "tribe" (2022) a "new society" (2023) y "town square of the new digital society" (2024).
- fusiones (unidades sustancialmente idénticas; usa la unión de su contenido): ninguna

### U-012-063

- **titulo:** Social media is the new town square; creators are the new Renaissance men; "creator" as the essence of your being, not an internet job
- **desarrollo:** Social media is the new town square; creators are the new Renaissance men, the decentralized education system and the new economy. "Stop thinking of a creator as some new internet job think of it as a part of life." "If you think of Creator as content creator you're doomed if you think of Creator as just the essence of your being you're going to win" — you're a creative, spiritual being, "a piece of a Creator". You already spend most of your day on your phone; acting like social media isn't ingrained in humanity is foolish. "Being a Creator is just expressing your value in the digital world rather than being a slave to it on the sidelines."
- **ejemplos:** ninguno
- **cita:** "if you think of Creator as content creator you're doomed if you think of Creator as just the essence of your being you're going to win"
- **terminos:** new town square, new Renaissance men, decentralized education system
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** fundamental
- **prerrequisitos:** U-012-042

- **desarrollo:** People don't realize how revolutionary social media is: it's where we learn (school), hire (careers), teach (business), and meet socially. We already live in the virtual world—people anticipate VR, but "we're already in it." Being a mindless consumer is like the WALL-E scene of people brainless in armchairs being fed cheap pleasure. Once you break out and see you can leverage it to contribute and help others break out, you see that "by neglecting social media you are neglecting The evolutionary path to your fullest potential"; its integration into our lives is a stepping stone to the next phase of human evolution.
- **ejemplos:** WALL-E armchair scene; virtual reality.
- **cita:** "social media is not just an app it's a new Society"

### U-007-202
- **tipo:** argumento
- **titulo:** Personal brands are the people of the new society; social media is a public job board, school, note-taking system and party
- **desarrollo:** If social media is the new society, personal brands are its people. Not limited to business owners: you have a social media account, you're just a consumer not contributing. It's global; not like a physical lot with only 100 buildings: you have your own lot; want digital real estate? sign up for a website builder and put up a website. Creators and brands are hiring people who display their value through their public resume (profile or content). "Social media is a public job board public school public note taking system and public party" where you find friends and nurture business relationships. A good start: work for another creator or brand to get real-world results with modern skills; every skill you apply to your own brand is applicable to others' brands, so building your own stacks the most applicable, best-paid knowledge.

## CLÚSTER C-T10b-03: Social media is the devil or the vessel (conscious use)
- idea: La red no es buena ni mala en sí: es "el diablo o el vehículo de tu potencial" según cómo la percibas, y esa percepción depende de tu desarrollo personal (si no te identificas como creador no ves oportunidades en un post viral o en la queja de un dueño de negocio). Es un reflejo de ti: el algoritmo te da lo que alimentas con tu atención, y puedes salir de ella como de un bar. Al mismo tiempo, el autor reconoce que multiplica el condicionamiento y la exposición a información polarizante; su respuesta es un uso consciente: menos de 30 minutos al día, contenido creado fuera de la plataforma, seguir cuentas curadas para inspiración e investigación, pasar de largo lo tóxico y usarla como acceso a los mejores maestros.
- nivel: intermedio
- evolución: del tono crítico de 2022 (conditioning, "worse all around": U-027-016, U-027-018) al tono afirmativo de 2023–2025 (reflection of yourself, vessel, engine of success). Ver "Evolución y tensiones".
- fusiones (unidades sustancialmente idénticas; usa la unión de su contenido): ninguna

### U-021-029
- **tipo:** principio
- **titulo:** Social media is the devil or the vessel for your potential depending on how you perceive it; perception of opportunity depends on self-development
- **desarrollo:** "Social media is the devil if you perceive it that way and social media is the vessel for your potential if you perceive it that way. How you perceive opportunities depends on your self-development." You won't see a viral post as inspiration if you don't identify as a writer or creator; you won't see a business owner's complaint post as an opportunity to reach out and offer your skills for money. "You're letting opportunities pass you by day in and day out just because you don't identify as someone who uses social media for work." You haven't built your mental body by developing a skill set that would allow it; you developed the skill set schools told you to so you could be "trained directly into a job." He sees social media "as virtual reality to create your potential."
- **ejemplos:** A viral post as inspiration; a business owner's complaint as a client opportunity.
- **cita:** "how you perceive opportunities depends on your self-development"

- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** fundamental

### U-011-198
- **tipo:** argumento
- **titulo:** Social media is a reflection of yourself: your attention conditions the algorithm and your brain
- **desarrollo:** People say social media is toxic and we'd be better without it — "yes in some cases, it's all relative" — but they don't realize "social media is a reflection of yourself because the algorithm gives you what you want to see." By giving attention, falling into negativity bias and enjoying toxicity, you condition the algorithm and therefore your brain into an echo chamber of toxicity. His feed isn't toxic: he learns and gets new ideas daily. If you don't like something: scroll past, block, unfollow. Humans are social creatures and social media removes barriers of socialization through media.
- **ejemplos:** His own feed.
- **cita:** "social media is a reflection of yourself because the algorithm gives you what you want to see"

### U-027-186

- **titulo:** Social media is like a bar you're free to leave—and you can visit one on the other side of the world
- **desarrollo:** People email asking if they must use social media, since it's bad for your mind. Yes, "if you let it be", if you can't control yourself and whom you follow. Otherwise it's "the greatest way to make friends in the world." Saying social media forces bad experiences is like going to a bar and acting like you must stay though you dislike the people; you're free to leave anytime—and on social media you can go to a bar on the other side of the world.

### U-027-016
- **tipo:** argumento
- **titulo:** Social media multiplies conditioning, making it ten times easier to become someone else
- **desarrollo:** If the brain is conditioned by the information/stimuli we receive, then before phones we were exposed only to parents, friends, teachers, people on the street, maybe news media. Now we check social media for hours (he urges checking your own screen time), exposing ourselves to many ideas; depending on how many we pay attention to, digest and resonate with—and most people don't question them, accepting them as law—we become even further from ourselves. It is "10 times easier" to be conditioned into a negative, toxic human being. He self-qualifies: maybe he sees it that way because social media has conditioned him to think the world is bad—"so I have some work to do myself."
- **ejemplos:** screen time check.
- **cita:** "we're in an age where it is 10 times easier to be conditioned"
- **terminos:** conditioning; socialization chamber

### U-027-018
- **tipo:** metáfora
- **titulo:** You'd cringe at being locked in a room with your friends for three days, yet you don't cringe at social media's hundredfold exposure
- **desarrollo:** Whether introvert or extrovert, you'd cringe at being locked in a room with all your friends for three days; you want to see them maybe two or three times, or once a week, for one to three hours. Yet you don't cringe at logging onto social media where there are a hundred times more people posting far more polarizing information. Your mind doesn't know any different—it's the same information, maybe in a different medium; in person you can at least see the person (transcript ambiguous: "you can see the bottle"). Social media is "worse all around."
- **ejemplos:** locked in a room with friends for three days.
- **cita:** "you don't cringe at the fact that you log onto social media where there's a hundred times more people posting much more polarizing information"

### U-027-019
- **tipo:** proceso
- **titulo:** Koe's social media usage: under 30 minutes a day, content created outside the platform, curated follows for inspiration and research
- **desarrollo:** Responding to "isn't your job to be on social media?", Koe says he talks about the bad aspects of social media. He is on social media less than 30 minutes a day; all his content is created outside social media; he logs on to engage with people and "get what I need" and that's it. He is very careful curating who he follows; it's not for mindless consumption but for inspiration and research. Recommended mindset: log on, get what you need, talk to people you need to network with, take advantage of the benefits and mass reach, and leave the rest—"the only other option is to distract yourself." He also explains he may not answer DMs/comments because he spends most of his time in his community helping those who want help most, and he couldn't create this content if he were on social media more—he'd just be copying everyone unconsciously because they'd be feeding his brain what they want him to say.
- **ejemplos:** his community as where he spends his time.
- **cita:** "I'm on social media less than 30 minutes a day all of my content is created outside of social media"
- **terminos:** mindless consumption; inspiration and research
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** intermedio

- **desarrollo:** Dan says (admitting he's bragging) that social media "has rarely been toxic or inflammatory to me": when he sees something toxic he scrolls past it, though some of it surely sticks and influences him. The mindset: collect valuable skill and insight, have "as many good and valuable inputs into your head as you want just through searching and curating," then "produce valuable outputs so you can contribute back to it," "turn social media into something better by your own personal efforts" and help others meanwhile.

## CLÚSTER C-T10b-04: You are the media (one-person media company)
- idea: Los medios son el front end de internet y forman el tejido social; hoy "you are the media" y tu negocio online es una one-person media company. Las redes permiten que una sola persona atraiga y monetice una audiencia (un canal de distribución) sin ceder el control a una editorial o un sello discográfico; el trabajo del dueño de negocio es poner su producto donde está la atención. Los creadores "atomizan" lo que hicieron las redes (capturar atención, formar comunidades, distribuir medios, generar ingresos). Frente a la avalancha de contenido procesado, la única salida es contribuir con contenido orgánico. Retirarse "a vivir en el bosque" no es tan noble espiritualmente como parece.
- nivel: fundamental
- evolución: ninguna (de "you could consider yourself media", 2023, a "you are the media" como respuesta a la saturación por IA, 2025).
- fusiones (unidades sustancialmente idénticas; usa la unión de su contenido): ninguna

Media includes video, writing and their forms; you must understand media to make any income. "You could consider yourself media... your business online a one-person media company." Media is how we learn, get new information, communicate business value; it consumes much of our lives and "forms a large amount of the social fabric that we operate on as a civilization."

### U-011-201
- **tipo:** fuente-de-tercero
- **titulo:** Matt Mike's three layers of social media value: platforms → apps → creators
- **desarrollo:** A Twitter thread by Matt Mike added new perspective to his theory. Three layers by value created (income and audience): (1) Platforms — first form of digital media, e.g., the phone; Google and Apple rule; trillions of dollars, billions of users. (2) Apps — Twitter, Instagram, TikTok, YouTube; captured the same order of value. (3) Creators — the layer most people miss because they're sucked into apps; huge creators (Rogan, MrBeast) are predicted to generate trillions and have billions of followers, becoming media companies with insane leverage. Smaller ones don't need that: "you need a million dollars a year and maybe 200,000 followers." "Creators are atomizing what the social networks did by capturing mass attention, forming communities, distributing media and generating revenue."
- **ejemplos:** Joe Rogan; MrBeast; $1M/year with 200K followers.
- **cita:** "creators are atomizing what the social networks did"
- **terminos:** three layers; platforms; apps; creator layer; atomizing

- **nivel:** intermedio

## CLÚSTER C-T10b-05: The publishing model broke (gatekeepers vs selling it yourself)
- idea: Aporte de Devon Eriksen: en el siglo XX las editoriales eran esencialmente venture capitalists (financiaban tiradas, daban adelantos, el midlist incubaba a futuros A-listers). Internet y la captura ideológica rompieron el modelo: dejaron de filtrar por calidad, perdieron valor para el público y luego para los autores (desaparecen adelantos, promoción y midlist). Su respuesta: demostrar primero que puedes vender y evaluar cualquier trato por lo que agrega (rechazó un adelanto de 5.000 USD y recaudó unos 43.000 en Kickstarter). Consejo a autores: la única forma de saber si tu obra vale es intentar venderla tú mismo; "no es tu decisión si tu obra es suficientemente buena, es de ellos".
- nivel: intermedio
- evolución: ninguna
- fusiones (unidades sustancialmente idénticas; usa la unión de su contenido): ninguna

### U-006-064
- **tipo:** argumento
- **titulo:** Twentieth-century publishers were essentially venture capitalists, and the midlist was the incubator for future A-listers.
- **desarrollo:** Devon Eriksen considered traditional publishing when he finished his first novel and weighed all options. During the 20th century there was a real need for publishers: you needed offset print runs, "which typically had a five fig cost associated with them," and you needed a financier. "A publisher was essentially a venture capitalist": they paid for printing, gave you an advance, enabled people starting out to make a living as writers, "and then they were going to make back their money on the ones that took off." "It was a working viable business model that provided value for everybody involved authors Publishers and readers." The "midlist is an incubator for your future a-listers that's what that exists for." (Dan Koe asked about Devon's use of "Manhattan," which Devon says "is not a compliment.")
- **ejemplos:** Offset print runs with five-figure cost; advances; the midlist.
- **cita:** "a publisher was essentially a venture capitalist during that time"
- **terminos:** midlist; Manhattan
- **origen:** de-tercero:Devon Eriksen
- **nivel:** intermedio
- **prerrequisitos:** ninguno
- **fuente:** The Most Important Skill To Learn In The Next 10 Years With Devon Eriksen.md, 1:33:32–1:34:56; 1:36:05, 2024-12-08
- **tension:** ninguna


### U-006-065
- **tipo:** argumento
- **titulo:** How the publishing model broke: the internet plus ideological capture made publishers filter for political alignment instead of quality, losing value for audiences and then for authors.
- **desarrollo:** Devon Eriksen: "there were two things that broke that and the first was the internet and the second was" ideological capture (in his polemical words, "gay race communism"). Publishing companies conglomerated in "the same 10 blocks of Manhattan," going to the same parties and social circles, became "less and less interested in what people wanted to read and more and more interested in what they thought people should read." Chain: (1) the function publishers provided to the audience was gatekeeping — "filter out all the trash"; (2) they started "filtering for aligned political opinions" instead of quality; (3) they no longer provided the benefit to the audience; (4) their business model became less profitable; (5) advances went away, promotional spending (especially for new authors) went away, they got rid of the midlist; (6) "because they weren't able to serve a purpose for the audience they also lost the ability to serve a purpose for the authors." Conclusion: "nowadays I don't think book publishers really serve any purpose at all for most genres and types of books."
- **ejemplos:** Disappearing advances, promotion and midlist.
- **cita:** "they were no longer filtering for Quality they were filtering for aligned political opinions"
- **terminos:** gatekeep; midlist; Manhattan
- **origen:** de-tercero:Devon Eriksen
- **nivel:** intermedio
- **prerrequisitos:** U-006-064
- **fuente:** The Most Important Skill To Learn In The Next 10 Years With Devon Eriksen.md, 1:34:56–1:37:29, 2024-12-08
- **tension:** ninguna


### U-006-067
- **tipo:** historia
- **titulo:** "Rewrite it in third person": the acquisitions editor who didn't know perspective and tense are tools, and the moment Devon chose self-publishing.
- **desarrollo:** Devon Eriksen's editor (whom he hired himself) enthusiastically showed sample chapters to an acquisitions person at a major publishing house. He read two or three sentences, held the folder out "as if I had offered him a dead spider," and said "rewrite it in third person you do know how to write in third person don't you." Devon realized this man "had no idea that perspective and tense are tools in an author's toolbox"; he would have had to teach him why Theft of Fire is written in first person present tense and "the precise technical craftsmanship reasons why that unusual Choice was made," if he could even be taught, "because he had the self-limiting belief I am an editor with 20 years of experience." Devon's line: "you repeated the same mistakes for 20 years and called it experience." The industry was "full of people who were very full of themselves" and of self-limiting identity beliefs; he "had to go and prove that I could sell."
- **ejemplos:** Theft of Fire's first person present tense.
- **cita:** "you repeated the same mistakes for 20 years and called it experience"
- **terminos:** perspective and tense are tools; self-limiting identity beliefs
- **origen:** de-tercero:Devon Eriksen
- **nivel:** complementario
- **prerrequisitos:** U-006-033
- **fuente:** The Most Important Skill To Learn In The Next 10 Years With Devon Eriksen.md, 1:39:42–1:40:54, 2024-12-08
- **tension:** ninguna


### U-006-068
- **tipo:** caso
- **titulo:** Prove you can sell first; then evaluate deals by what they add — Devon declined a $5,000 audiobook advance and raised about $43,000 on Kickstarter.
- **desarrollo:** After Devon Eriksen proved he could sell, publishers came with deals. His evaluation: "this deal you're offering me isn't very good because the only additional feature that this offers to me is you get paid... that's not a feature." People offered him "like a $5,000 advance to sell them the audiobook rights." Instead he ran a Kickstarter to fund the audiobook, raising "I think $43,000," hired three very talented professional actors, one for each major role, and did it "with all the bells and whistles." It confirmed his belief: "I don't need someone to give me permission to create I can just create things and if they're good enough people will be interested."
- **ejemplos:** $5,000 audiobook advance vs. ~$43,000 Kickstarter; three voice actors.
- **cita:** "the only additional feature that this offers to me is you get paid"
- **terminos:** ninguno
- **origen:** de-tercero:Devon Eriksen
- **nivel:** intermedio
- **prerrequisitos:** U-006-066
- **fuente:** The Most Important Skill To Learn In The Next 10 Years With Devon Eriksen.md, 1:40:54–1:43:11, 2024-12-08
- **tension:** ninguna


### U-006-070
- **tipo:** heurística
- **titulo:** Advice to aspiring authors: the way to find out whether your work has value is to try to sell it yourself — it's not your decision whether it's good enough, it's theirs.
- **desarrollo:** Devon Eriksen speaks directly to aspiring authors: "I know you're afraid I was afraid too everyone is afraid... you stay afraid even as you start having success the fear is constant." You don't know if your work has value; friends and relatives who read it and said "wow this is really good" may just be trying to be nice. "The way you find out is to try to sell it yourself": if it has value, as you get out there "flogging it and persuading a few people to read it," they will say it's good and recommend it to friends. "It won't be a fast process but it does work," and it's "so much better as a measure of value than whether some Acquisitions guy at a Publishing House is going to compliment you," because they don't have "any special magic knowledge or Insight that you do not have." "People are out there they want good stuff and they will buy it if you have the courage to offer it to them... it's not your decision whether your work is good enough it's theirs give them the opportunity to make that decision."
- **ejemplos:** Friends' and relatives' feedback vs. market feedback.
- **cita:** "it's not your decision whether your work is good enough it's theirs give them the opportunity to make that decision"
- **terminos:** measure of value
- **origen:** de-tercero:Devon Eriksen
- **nivel:** fundamental
- **prerrequisitos:** U-006-056; U-006-066
- **fuente:** The Most Important Skill To Learn In The Next 10 Years With Devon Eriksen.md, 1:43:11–1:45:24, 2024-12-08
- **tension:** ninguna



## CLÚSTER C-T14b-04: Virtual society and decentralized media (centralization/decentralization cycle)
- idea: Las redes sociales son una "virtual society" que surgió de resolver problemas de localidad (comunicación, información, negocio y oportunidad eran locales): la evolución es "a slow burn until it finally explodes", no la sociedad VR imaginada. El autor lo enmarca en un ciclo natural de centralización y descentralización (el océano que se evapora y vuelve como lluvia): de ~300 canales de TV que moldeaban la identidad a miles de creadores que permiten elegir qué y cómo aprender; los creadores son "decentralized media companies" y componen "the new public school system". Cree que las redes nacieron de un deseo profundo de conexión, de compartir conocimiento y resolver problemas a escala, aunque las distracciones superficiales lo taparon; quien lo ve, encuentra en esta "global decentralized economy" un vehículo para su life's work.
- nivel: intermedio
- evolución: ninguna
- fusiones (unidades sustancialmente idénticas; usa la unión de su contenido): ninguna

### U-007-191
- **tipo:** argumento
- **titulo:** A new virtual society emerged (social media) by solving burning problems of locality; evolution is a slow burn until it explodes
- **desarrollo:** A new, virtual society has emerged, not the one people imagined years down the road via something like Apple's new VR headset; "that's not how Evolution Works". Evolution comes from solving the problems that cause our suffering: a slow burn until it explodes. The new society came "as fast as a star exploding"; we called it social media. Programmers, visionaries and businessmen spent years solving burning problems: until then communication, information, business and opportunities were local. Centuries of geographic restriction changed in the blink of an eye. The internet allowed social media; social media allowed the creator economy, so new that few grasp its significance.
- **ejemplos:** Apple's VR headset as the expected-but-wrong vision
- **cita:** "Evolution comes from solving the problems that cause our suffering it is a slow burn until it finally explodes"
- **terminos:** virtual society; creator economy; geographic restriction
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** intermedio
- **prerrequisitos:** none
- **fuente:** The One Person Business Model 2.0 (Turn Yourself Into A Business).md, 0:00-1:00, 2023-06-25
- **tension:** ninguna


### U-012-024
- **tipo:** concepto
- **titulo:** The new economy as a cycle of centralization and decentralization; creators as decentralized media and the new public school system
- **desarrollo:** "The universe divides and reunites like the ocean evaporates and condenses back down into rain" — he calls this a cycle of centralization and decentralization. Social media caused a shift from centralized to decentralized media. Synthesizers/creators are decentralized media companies; they "compose the new public school system that is available to anyone with an internet connection". Individuals are creating new jobs, teaching new information and creating new paths that schools and governments can't keep up with.
- **ejemplos:** ocean evaporating and condensing into rain
- **cita:** "synthesizers or creators are decentralized media companies they compose the new public school system"
- **terminos:** cycle of centralization and decentralization, new economy
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** intermedio
- **prerrequisitos:** U-012-020
- **fuente:** The Future Of Work Is Play (How To Create A Digital Career).md, 19:40, 2023-12-31
- **tension:** ninguna


### U-012-103
- **tipo:** argumento
- **titulo:** From ~300 TV channels to thousands of creators: why the creator economy can change society
- **desarrollo:** In the past not everyone could have a radio show or TV channel. People had around 300 TV channels (his recollection: "it's like 300 right"), watched hours a day, and that narrow information shaped identity and perceived potential. Thousands upon thousands of creators let people choose what and how they learn and create a more optimal environment for the value provided through media channels. This is why he's passionate about the creator economy as a way to change society.
- **ejemplos:** ~300 TV channels in the US
- **cita:** ninguna

- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** complementario

### U-015-061
- **tipo:** opinión
- **titulo:** Social media was born from a deeper desire for human connection, knowledge sharing and solving problems at scale
- **desarrollo:** Dan believes social media was created from "a deeper desire for human connection, sharing your knowledge and solving problems at scale." Because shallow distractions took over, few people see it as a vessel to pursue their life's work. "You have indirect access to nearly everyone in the world and you don't think any of them can benefit from what you have to offer?" It takes development and skill acquisition, but once you see the potential for everyone to have a business in "this global decentralized economy," it's hard to pursue any other path.

# SECCIÓN 25.6 — Plataformas y concentration of force


## CLÚSTER C-T10a-21: Choosing a platform (start with one writing-based platform)
- idea: No hay forma de evitar crear contenido: el valor se distribuye por medios. El autor recomienda empezar en una plataforma de escritura y quedarse en una sola hasta dominar el crecimiento. En 2022 eligió Twitter: es "an idea platform" (no necesitas imágenes ni ser modelo, la escritura es la base de todo), el botón de retweet es compartir de baja fricción, y los comentarios funcionan como foro donde se ve tu marca. En 2023 sumó Threads (comunidad cruda, repost de baja fricción, "reply reply reply", publicar 1–3 veces al día "para sacar los fracasos del camino"). En 2024 propone elegir entre X, Threads o LinkedIn según con cuál resuenas y a cuál entras más, no según lo fácil que sea crecer hoy, porque eso sube y baja; LinkedIn es "blue ocean", con gente con dinero y sin trolls. Primeras ideas de contenido: tu historia y transformación, enseñar tus habilidades e intereses, problemas y soluciones, lecciones aprendidas, ideas de libros y podcasts hechas tuyas.
- nivel: intermedio
- evolución: la plataforma recomendada cambia (Twitter en 2022, Threads en 2023, X/Threads/LinkedIn a elección en 2024), y el criterio pasa de "dónde es más fácil crecer" a "dónde vas a quedarte". Ver "Evolución y tensiones".
- fusiones (unidades sustancialmente idénticas; usa la unión de su contenido): ninguna

### U-011-105
- **tipo:** método
- **titulo:** Packaging knowledge, step 1: choose a media platform — why the author recommends Twitter
- **desarrollo:** You distribute value via media; "that's all the internet is"; there's no way around creating content. He prefers Twitter: he started there; many in his network grew there; it's "underrated and untapped." Before, he tried a fitness YouTube channel, a digital art Instagram page, paid ads, SEO — missing crucial pieces — but on Twitter "everything just clicked." Reasons: (a) you need a profile picture but no images; it's "an idea platform," for writing and clear articulation — the foundation of everything; people screenshot tweets onto other platforms because the message is valuable and they often outperform other posts (vs. "an Instagram model in a bikini"); you just need to type and some branding/marketing knowledge for a profile that makes people follow. (b) The retweet button: one click to share, versus much higher friction elsewhere; growth = getting eyes on content → profile → follow. "This isn't YouTube" where content builds up via SEO; put 90–100% of effort on getting good content in front of people. (c) Comments: Twitter is more of a forum; your comment shows your profile picture, name, branding and that you added value, unlike Instagram's username-plus-line. "Writing is the foundation of everything": compelling tweets improve all other content.
- **ejemplos:** His failed fitness YouTube, digital art Instagram, paid ads, SEO; screenshotted tweets.
- **cita:** "it's an idea platform"
- **terminos:** idea platform; retweet button; friction
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** intermedio
- **prerrequisitos:** U-011-104
- **fuente:** The Rise Of The Value Creator (A Career Path For Polymaths & Self-Improvers).md, 23:12–26:32, 2022-10-31
- **tension:** ninguna


### U-011-237
- **tipo:** método
- **titulo:** Why Threads (2023) and the basics of growing on Twitter/Threads
- **desarrollo:** Twitter was and is underrated; Threads reminds him of Twitter in its infancy — "a raw community," fun. Why: post as much as you like to test ideas before other platforms; writing-based, so you don't need to be "a 10 out of 10 model living in Thailand"; a forum where you find like-minded people in replies of favorite accounts; repost button is low-friction sharing; replies heavily favored ("reply reply reply"); screenshot tweets/threads to grow on other platforms without hours of new content. Basics: post 1–3 times a day and "start getting your failures out of the way"; under 1,000 followers, network in replies and DMs to leverage others' audiences; clickable profile picture so people know you're a creator; bio = your big goal or the interest you talk about, what you lead people toward and how through your unique interests; write threads/long posts on your story and personal experience but have a network to share it so it goes semi-viral; start with beginner-level, actionable educational content (YouTubers' histories show actionable advice on their expertise with personality baked in).
- **ejemplos:** Threads launch; YouTubers' early videos.
- **cita:** "start getting your failures out of the way"
- **terminos:** low friction share; replies; clickable profile picture
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** intermedio
- **prerrequisitos:** U-011-235
- **fuente:** The Value Creator (A New Internet Career Path For Intelligent People).md, 26:51–28:14, 2023-07-19
- **tension:** Updates U-011-105 (2022, Twitter as recommended platform) toward Threads in 2023.

- **desarrollo:** Three options: X, Threads, LinkedIn, each with pros and cons. LinkedIn has the most professionals and probably the most money to make. Threads is like Instagram culture, with quite a few trolls, but engagement currently seems good for beginners. All platforms go up and down in ease of growth; "picking a platform based off of how easy it is to grow isn't a good long term strategy." Pick one you like and will stick with — the one you log onto most.

Another benefit: no trolls, because everyone fears losing their job if they troll in someone's comments, which, he adds, is another reason to start a business so you don't have to worry about that.

## CLÚSTER C-T10a-33: Concentration of force, then branch out with validated ideas
- idea: Hay más en el crecimiento que el contenido: publicar en todas las plataformas a la vez quita tiempo para conexiones; conviene concentrarse en una y usar ese seguimiento como leverage (el autor ofreció a cuentas grandes de Instagram compartirse mutuamente con su audiencia de X). Una vez validadas las ideas en una plataforma, se llevan a otras sabiendo que generan crecimiento: así creció en Instagram "10 veces más rápido que nadie". Umbrales distintos según la versión: unas 10 ideas que funcionen, 50,000 seguidores como señal de que sabes lo que haces, o haber reemplazado ingresos; el autor revisó sus mejores 100–200 ideas (300–400 de 40,000 tweets) para Instagram, y pasó de $800,000 al año con X a 4 millones con una audiencia diversificada. Otra rama: el podcast a los ~50K seguidores para conocer gente de alto nivel (Justin Welsh). Las animaciones lo llevaron de 250K a más de 1 millón en Instagram.
- nivel: intermedio
- evolución: el umbral para diversificar cambia según el video (unas 10 ideas validadas en 2023; 50,000 seguidores en 2024-09; tras ser rentable en 2024-06; la fuente de U-009-246 lo contrasta con U-009-112, otro bloque, que no da número). Ver "Evolución y tensiones".
- fusiones (unidades sustancialmente idénticas; usa la unión de su contenido): FUSION: U-013-032 <= U-001-097 | texto prácticamente idéntico del paso 9 ("branch out to other platforms with validated ideas") del video de 2022 reutilizado en la guía de 2024; se conserva el original

### U-022-139
- **tipo:** caso
- **titulo:** Concentration of force on social media: the author focused solely on X, then used that following to cross-promote and grow fast on Instagram.
- **desarrollo:** "There is more to growth than just content, so cross-posting to all platforms takes away time you could be spending building connections with people that will help you grow. You'd gain more followers by focusing on one platform first, which can then be used as leverage to grow on the other platforms." The author chose Twitter/X as his first platform, focused solely on it, built connections he still talks to. With a large X following, when deploying on Instagram he messaged people with large Instagram followings: "Hey, I have a large following on X. Do you want to help each other grow by sharing each other's content on either platform?" "That alone allowed me to grow on Instagram so much faster than anyone else just starting from scratch." (Taught in week four of his Creator Bootcamp.)
- **ejemplos:** X → Instagram cross-sharing.

Kickstart podcast, YouTube or Instagram audiences by telling your current audience to follow and share. Port your best ideas: after growing on Twitter he looked back at his best 100–200 ideas, posted them on Instagram with a growth strategy, and grew faster without repeating the trial and error — "if only 300 or 400 posts do really well out of the 40,000 that I wrote on Twitter" those are what to transfer. As platforms grow, people download free products, read newsletters, buy products: "exponential growth kicks in" — you can double, triple or quadruple income in a year.

He could keep inviting higher-level people who then help him grow.
